266 — Browser Sandbox / Multi-Process Architecture

Goal: Implement, from scratch in Rust on Linux, a Chromium-style multi-process browser architecture and sandbox — a privileged browser/broker process that spawns and supervises untrusted child processes (renderer, GPU, network, utility), a Mojo-like typed IPC layer over UNIX domain sockets with FD/handle passing (SCM_RIGHTS) and shared memory, OS-level renderer confinement (seccomp-bpf syscall filtering; user/pid/net/mount/ipc/uts namespaces; pivot_root; capability dropping; no_new_privs), a pre-forked zygote fork-server, a capability-validating resource broker, site isolation (process-per-site-instance keyed on scheme + eTLD+1), crash isolation and recovery, per-process resource budgets, and a malicious-renderer red-team harness. The Linux backend is primary; macOS (Seatbelt) and Windows (job objects / AppContainer) backends are gated behind PhaseDeferred. It builds the process model and the sandbox; it is not a web engine — the renderer is a minimal untrusted stand-in (real rendering is #260 / #305).

Project Number: 266

Project Name: Browser Sandbox / Multi-Process Architecture

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

Goal: Build, from scratch in Rust on Linux, a Chromium-style multi-process browser process model + OS sandbox — a privileged browser/broker (the TCB) that spawns and confines fully-untrusted child processes behind a hard trust boundary, proven by a red-team harness.

1.1 What is built (engineering definition)

A privilege-separated application sandbox + process-model substrate. The browser (broker) process is the Trusted Computing Base (TCB); renderer/GPU/network/utility children are untrusted, OS-confined, and reach every resource only via deny-by-default brokering. Nine load-bearing subsystems:

# Subsystem Crate(s) One-line definition
1 Multi-process model sbx-browser, sbx-launcher Privileged browser spawns + supervises ProcessType-typed children; owns all ProcessHosts.
2 Typed IPC sbx-ipc Mojo-like MessagePipe over UNIX domain sockets; Message{header,payload,handles}; Interface+Endpoint<T>; req/resp + async.
3 Handle + shared memory sbx-ipc, sbx-shm FD passing via SCM_RIGHTS (Handle); memfd-backed SharedBuffer/Mapping/ShmRing + seals for zero-copy.
4 OS sandbox (Linux) sbx-sandbox SeccompFilter (cBPF over seccomp_data) + user/pid/net/mnt/ipc/uts Namespaces + pivot_root + Rlimits + no_new_privs + cap-drop.
5 Zygote sbx-zygote Pre-forked, pre-sandboxed Zygote; ForkServer protocol; COW page sharing → cheap renderer spawn; per-child seccomp seal.
6 Resource broker sbx-broker FileBroker/SocketBroker/ResourceBroker; PolicyEngine validates each BrokerRequest vs the caller CapabilitySet; returns brokered FDs.
7 Site isolation sbx-site SiteInstance/BrowsingInstance; site = scheme + eTLD+1 (public suffix list); process-per-site-instance + ProcessReusePolicy.
8 Crash isolation + recovery sbx-crash CrashHandler (SIGCHLD/exit/IPC-disconnect) → Minidump + RestartPolicy + "sad tab"; renderer death ≠ browser death.
9 Red-team + observability sbx-cli, Phase 13 Malicious-renderer harness proves forbidden syscalls/files/sockets are denied/killed; per-process budgets + metrics + tracing.

Two independent enforcement layers (defense in depth): (a) the seccomp-bpf syscall filter, (b) the broker policy. A single layer's bug must not yield a sandbox escape — every renderer OS access is either denied by seccomp or brokered + validated against its CapabilitySet.

1.2 System boundaries

  • In scope: the process model, IPC + handle/shm passing, the Linux OS confinement stack, zygote spawn, the resource broker + capability model, site isolation, crash recovery, resource budgets/observability, and the security-validation harness.
  • Trust boundary: runs between the browser/broker (privileged, in-TCB) and every child. Children are assumed fully compromised; correctness depends only on TCB code + the kernel.
  • TCB surface: sbx-browser + sbx-broker + sbx-launcher + sbx-zygote (parent side) + the parsing/validation paths of sbx-ipc/sbx-sandbox. Memory-safety + input-validation rigor concentrates here; this is why the language is Rust (§1.7).
  • Untrusted input: all bytes arriving from a child over sbx-ipc (malformed Message, bogus handle counts, oversized payloads) are adversarial; the broker must never crash, leak, or over-grant on them.

1.3 NON-goals (explicit)

Not built here Why / owner
Real web rendering / layout / paint → #260 Browser Rendering Pipeline, #305 Browser Rendering Engine.
JavaScript engine / runtime → #305; the renderer runs no script.
sbx-renderer as a real browser engine It is a minimal untrusted stand-in that exercises the model (navigate, broker a resource, force a crash).
A network stack (TCP/IP, TLS, HTTP) → #33 / #64; the network process here only frames the brokering boundary.
Generic container runtime / image mgmt → #35; this is a single-purpose application sandbox, not a container manager.
Hypervisor / VM isolation → #153.
Full sandboxed-browser security-model writeup Related framing in #337 Sandboxed Browser Security Model; here it is the mechanism, not the survey.

1.4 Deployment model

Three artifacts from one workspace:

  1. Linux desktop-class browser shell — sbx run boots a browser process + minimal renderer children, end-to-end through navigation → SiteInstance → spawn → broker → crash recovery.
  2. Embeddable library set — the sbx-* crates as a reusable process-model + sandbox toolkit for other privilege-separated apps.
  3. The sbx CLI — subcommands run, spawn, policy, ipc-ping, sandbox-test, crash-test, sites, attack (red-team harness).

1.5 Target users

Browser-platform engineers; OS-security / sandbox authors; anyone building privilege-separated multi-process systems on Linux who needs a memory-safe broker astride an untrusted boundary. Assumes comfort with kernel syscall semantics and the browser security model.

1.6 Phase-0 deliverable — the twelve sbx-* crates

A Cargo workspace (edition 2021, MSRV 1.78), CI/Makefile/Dockerfile, Linux declared primary, and a smoke test (browser spawns a child; they exchange a hello Message over a UDS; child exits cleanly). The 12 members:

  1. sbx-core — OS-free shared types: ProcessId, ProcessType, ProcessRole, SiteId, BrowsingInstanceId, Capability, CapabilitySet, HandleId, RouteId, SbxError.
  2. sbx-ipc — Mojo-like IPC: MessagePipe, Message, wire framing, Interface/Endpoint<T>, Handle (over UDS).
  3. sbx-shm — shared memory: SharedBuffer (memfd), Mapping (ro/rw), ShmRing, seal flags.
  4. sbx-sandbox — Sandbox trait + LinuxSandbox; SeccompFilter, SyscallPolicy, Namespaces, pivot_root/rlimit/no_new_privs/cap-drop, Policy.
  5. sbx-zygote — Zygote, ForkServer + fork-request protocol, COW sharing, post-fork seal.
  6. sbx-broker — Broker trait + FileBroker/SocketBroker/ResourceBroker; BrokerRequest/BrokerResponse; PolicyEngine.
  7. sbx-launcher — ProcessLauncher, LaunchOptions, ChildProcess, ProcessHandle; spawn + handshake + monitor/reap/restart.
  8. sbx-site — SiteInstance, BrowsingInstance, SiteIsolationPolicy, ProcessReusePolicy; eTLD+1 via public suffix list.
  9. sbx-browser — BrowserProcess, ProcessHost, RenderProcessHost, the RouteId IPC router.
  10. sbx-renderer — minimal untrusted RendererProcess, RenderFrame; sandboxed; brokers resources; test crash hook.
  11. sbx-crash — CrashHandler, Minidump, RestartPolicy, SIGCHLD/exit watcher, sad-tab signal.
  12. sbx-cli — the sbx binary + subcommands above.

1.7 What later phases unlock

Phases Capability unlocked
1–2 (## 13–## 14) Typed IPC + SCM_RIGHTS handle/shm passing → the capability-passing primitive.
3 (## 15) clone/exec child lifecycle, handshake, SIGCHLD reap/restart (pre-sandbox).
4–5 (## 16–## 17) The full Linux confinement: namespaces + pivot_root + rlimits, then seccomp-bpf (the OS half of defense-in-depth).
6 (## 18) Fast COW renderer spawn via the zygote fork server.
7–8 (## 19–## 20) Broker + capability model → the brokered half of defense-in-depth; per-ProcessType least-privilege profiles.
9 (## 21) Site isolation: process-per-site-instance keyed on scheme+eTLD+1.
10 (## 22) Browser orchestrator: ProcessHost registry + RouteId multiplexing + navigation→process assignment.
11 (## 23) Crash blast-radius containment + sad tab + restart.
12 (## 24) Per-process budgets, priority, metrics, cross-boundary tracing.
13 (## 25) The security "done" gate: red-team harness + seccomp conformance + IPC fuzzing + perf benchmarks.

1.8 Deferred features (behind PhaseDeferred, "FINAL_BLUEPRINT section X")

  • macOS Seatbelt + Windows (job objects / restricted token / AppContainer) sandbox backends — stubs behind PhaseDeferred; Linux is primary.
  • GPU / Vulkan channel — only the relaxed-sandbox GPU process boundary is modeled; no real GPU command stream.
  • Full network-service brokering — SocketBroker validates capabilities; no real network stack (→ #33/#64).
  • A real renderer — kept a minimal stand-in (→ #260/#305).

1.9 Estimated timeline

Milestone Effort
Phase 0 (skeleton, 12 crates, smoke test) 6–14 weeks
Phases 1–13 (full process model + sandbox + site isolation + red-team gate) several months

Critical path: Phase 4–5 (the OS sandbox) and Phase 7–8 (the broker/capability model) — the two security layers gate the security "done" criterion in Phase 13.

1.10 Recommended language: Rust (edition 2021, MSRV 1.78)

std + the nix/libc syscall surface (raw fork/clone/exec/wait, seccomp, namespaces, SCM_RIGHTS, memfd) with safe wrappers over the handles. Rationale (TCB-centric): this is privilege-separation code on a trust boundary parsing input from a fully-untrusted renderer — Chromium's sandbox CVE history is dominated by C++ memory-unsafety in exactly this surface, and Rust removes that entire class from the TCB. Enums + exhaustive match model the Capability set and IPC MsgKind dispositions so a missing case is a compile error, not a silent over-grant. Ownership makes Handle close-on-drop semantics correct by construction (no FD leaks across the boundary). Precedent: Servo, Firefox's Rust IPC, and newer Chromium Rust components.

Rejected alternatives:

Lang Why rejected
C / C++ Chromium's language, but its memory-unsafety attack surface in the broker is precisely what we eliminate.
Go Runtime + M:N goroutine scheduler fights fork()/clone()/seccomp/namespace low-level control; GC + thread-per-filter friction.
Any GC scripting lang No syscall-level control; no place in a TCB doing seccomp/namespace setup.

1.11 Required prerequisites (Required Reading anchors)

  • Linux process model: fork/clone/exec/wait, process groups, SIGCHLD reaping — clone(2), wait(2).
  • Namespaces: unprivileged user namespace (fake root → create the rest without real privilege); pid/net/mnt/ipc/uts — user_namespaces(7), namespaces(7), pivot_root(2).
  • seccomp-bpf: cBPF over struct seccomp_data{nr,arch,instruction_pointer,args[6]}; SECCOMP_RET_* dispositions; arch-check first to block the x32/ia32 ABI bypass; install after PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS — seccomp(2), prctl(2).
  • Capabilities: capability dropping, no_new_privs — capabilities(7).
  • IPC: UNIX domain sockets + SCM_RIGHTS FD passing — unix(7).
  • Shared memory: mmap, memfd_create + seals — memfd_create(2), mmap(2).
  • Browser security model: Chromium multi-process architecture + Site Isolation + the Linux sandbox layers + zygote + Mojo; Firefox process model + IPDL + sandbox; minijail + gVisor (syscall filtering).

2. Why Build This

Build this to internalize the defining security architecture of the modern web: how a program runs fully untrusted, attacker-controlled code (a web page) on a user's machine — billions of times a day — and survives. The browser is the most-attacked software on most machines; its answer is not "find all the bugs" but architectural privilege separation — assume the renderer will be compromised and make that compromise worthless. This project builds the process model + sandbox that makes that assumption safe to hold, then attacks it (Phase 13 red-team harness) to prove it.

2.1 Unique systems knowledge this teaches

Domain What you actually learn (the implementation, not the theory)
Privilege separation across a hard trust boundary Split one program into a TCB (browser/broker) + untrusted principals (renderers) at a kernel-enforced line; audit every crossing "what if the far side lies?". The TRUST BOUNDARY line in the §3 diagram is the project.
OS sandboxing primitives seccomp-bpf: hand-build a cBPF program over struct seccomp_data{nr,arch,instruction_pointer,args[6]} with ALLOW/ERRNO/TRAP/KILL_PROCESS/LOG dispositions + arg filters. Namespaces: unprivileged USER ns → fake-root → create PID/NET/MNT/IPC/UTS without real privilege. pivot_root → minimal RO rootfs. PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS, rlimit, full capability drop — the exact primitives minijail/gVisor/Chromium use.
Capability-based brokering A renderer in a NET-less, pivot_root-jailed namespace cannot open()/connect(). It asks the broker; the PolicyEngine validates vs the caller's CapabilitySet and returns only an FD (never a path/host). Object-capability security, concretely.
The zygote / fork-server pattern Warm shared RO pages once, then fork() per renderer → COW sharing → cheap spawn vs cold exec. Each child seals (tightens) its seccomp policy post-fork before touching untrusted work.
Typed IPC + FD passing Mojo-like Interface/Endpoint<T> request/response over UNIX domain sockets; SCM_RIGHTS to duplicate FDs across the boundary (the capability-passing primitive); memfd-backed shared memory + seals + an SPSC ShmRing for zero-copy bulk transfer.
Site isolation as architecture site = scheme + eTLD+1 (registrable domain via public-suffix list); process-per-site-instance; SiteInstance/BrowsingInstance; cross-origin data lives in a different address space → a Spectre-class read or a UAF cannot reach it.
Crash isolation + blast-radius reasoning Renderer death detected via SIGCHLD/IPC-disconnect, reified as a Minidump, surfaced as a sad tab — browser + peers untouched. Reasoning about what a compromise can reach = exactly its CapabilitySet, no more.

Throughline: defense in depth = two independent layers — the seccomp filter and the broker policy. A single bug in either must not yield escape; you build both, then break them.

2.2 Production engineering concepts exercised

Deny-by-default policy engines; least-privilege per-ProcessType capability profiles; supervision (spawn/monitor/reap/restart with a bounded RestartPolicy); resource budgets (per-process rlimit + memory/CPU caps + process-count limits); cross-trust-boundary observability (metrics: process count, crash rate, IPC throughput, broker request rate, spawn latency; tracing across processes); fuzzing the adversarial input path (malformed Message → broker never crashes); conformance gating (seccomp policy + red-team harness as a CI "done" gate).

2.3 Why the multi-process browser matters (historical + architectural)

  • 2008 — Chrome multi-process design. The web shifted from documents to untrusted programs (JS, plugins). A monolithic browser meant one renderer bug = full machine compromise. Renderer-per-tab + sandbox demoted a renderer exploit to a foothold, not a win. This is the model.
  • 2018 — Site Isolation (post-Spectre). Spectre/Meltdown broke the in-process security assumption: a renderer could read its own address space across origins via speculative side channels. The fix was architectural, not a patch — put different sites in different processes, so there is no cross-origin secret in the address space to leak. A CPU-level catastrophe became a contained one. This project implements that exact principal model (SiteInstance keyed on scheme + eTLD+1).
  • Canonical prior art (cited consistently): Chromium's multi-process architecture, Site Isolation design, the Linux sandbox (SUID/namespace/seccomp-bpf layers), the zygote, Mojo IPC; Firefox's process model + IPDL + sandbox (Seatbelt/seccomp); minijail + gVisor (syscall filtering). Chromium's sandbox CVE history is dominated by C++ memory unsafety in the broker — the precise surface a Rust TCB removes. That is why this is Rust: the broker parses input from a fully hostile renderer; memory safety in the TCB is the point.

2.4 Engineering muscles built

  • Designing a trust boundary and auditing every crossing for "what if the other side lies?".
  • Direct clone(2)/fork()/execve orchestration; FD lifecycle + ownership across processes (Handle close-on-drop, no double-close, no leak across the boundary).
  • Writing a cBPF program by hand and reasoning about ABI bypasses (arch-check first → block x32/ia32).
  • Deny-by-default policy engines + least-privilege capability profiles per ProcessType.
  • Failure-mode-first design: crash detection, restart policy, blast-radius containment, "the renderer is already gone — now what?".
  • Building a red-team harness against your own system — proving forbidden syscalls/files are denied/killed, not assuming it.
  • Quantified systems targets: spawn latency (zygote vs cold), IPC throughput, per-process memory, broker request rate.

2.5 Cross-links to portfolio projects

Project Relationship
#28 OS Kernel Provides the namespace/seccomp/clone primitives this sandbox consumes — build one to understand the other side of the syscall.
#50 Sandbox / Code Execution Sibling sandbox; this is the application-specific (browser) instance of the general technique.
#154 eBPF Sandbox Same syscall-filtering family; seccomp-bpf is classic-BPF, eBPF the richer modern cousin.
#149 IPC Mechanisms sbx-ipc is a concrete security-hardened instance: typed messages, FD passing, shared memory.
#153 Hypervisor The next isolation tier (VM vs process); explicit non-goal here — process-level, not a hypervisor.
#35 Container Runtime Shares namespaces/pivot_root/cap-drop but is a general container manager; this is a single-purpose app sandbox (explicit non-goal to be a runtime).
#226 Secure Enclave / TPM Hardware-rooted trust boundary; complements this software-rooted one.
#260 Browser Rendering Pipeline / #305 Rendering Engine The real renderer that would run inside this sandbox; here the renderer is a minimal untrusted stand-in (explicit non-goal: no layout/JS).
#337 Sandboxed Browser Security Model Same security framing at the model/threat layer; this project is the concrete process-model + sandbox implementation.

2.6 Calibration value

Completing this calibrates you against the people who design browser/OS sandboxes for a living. You will have: hand-written seccomp filters, an unprivileged-namespace + pivot_root jail, a capability-validating broker, a zygote fork-server, FD-passing typed IPC, a site-isolation principal model, crash recovery, and an adversarial harness that demonstrates the sandbox holds. The deliverable is not "a browser" — it is the provable claim that an untrusted child, fully compromised, is contained to exactly its CapabilitySet, demonstrated against your own red team. That claim is the calibration signal, and the skill — privilege separation across a hostile boundary in a memory-safe TCB — transfers to every sandbox, runtime, and multi-process system you build after.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

  • Trust-boundary auditing (Phase 0–3): Given any IPC message arriving from a child, you can enumerate every assumption the broker makes and construct an adversarial input that would break each one if input validation were absent. You articulate why num_handles mismatch closes stray fds rather than ignoring them.
  • Hands-on confinement stack (Phase 4–5): You can write, from memory, the cBPF sequence that (a) checks seccomp_data.arch before the syscall number, (b) blocks x32/ia32 ABI reentry, and (c) applies KillProcess as the default disposition. You explain why PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS must precede filter installation and why unprivileged user namespaces remove the need for a SUID helper.
  • Capability-based brokering (Phase 7–8): You can design a PolicyEngine from scratch — define the CapabilitySet algebra, write the deny-by-default validation loop, and explain exactly why passing an FD (not a path) defeats the TOCTOU class identified in P11 (Garfinkel 2003).
  • Zygote fork-server and COW arithmetic (Phase 6): You can compute, from the §4 constraints, the expected private RSS delta per renderer and predict when COW pages will diverge into private dirty pages. You can describe the post-fork seccomp-seal sequence and why it must complete before untrusted data arrives.
  • Site isolation principal model (Phase 9): You can explain why eTLD+1 (not eTLD+2 or full origin) is the right site granularity, walk through the PSL wildcard-and-exception algorithm for a tricky case (*.ck vs www.ck), and explain why BrowsingInstance grouping overrides ProcessReusePolicy for script-reachable contexts.
  • Red-team and defense-in-depth proof (Phase 13): You can author an attack test case that verifies a forbidden syscall is killed, explain why a seccomp-filter bug alone is not a sandbox escape if the broker layer also denies the request, and articulate the blast-radius bound of a full renderer compromise in terms of CapabilitySet.
  • Scalability envelope (Scalability Analysis): You can derive the process-count ceiling from the §4 memory constraints, state the IPC fan-out O-notation of the RouteId router, and name the two mitigations (ShmRing offload + router sharding) that push past the single-loop bottleneck without weakening confinement.

3. Architectural Overview

The system is a privilege-separated process model split by a single hard line: the trust boundary. Everything above it is the TCB (the browser/broker process, written in safe Rust); everything below it is fully untrusted (renderer, GPU, network, utility children). The diagram below is canonical; the rest of the document elaborates each box.

+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                  Browser (broker) process   [TCB · Privileged]                |
|  sbx-browser: ProcessHost registry · RouteId IPC router                       |
|  sbx-site: SiteInstance/BrowsingInstance · site = scheme+eTLD+1 -> process     |
|  sbx-broker: File/Socket/Resource brokers · PolicyEngine (CapabilitySet)      |
|  sbx-launcher + sbx-zygote (fork server)   sbx-crash (SIGCHLD watch · restart) |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
      |              |               |                |                |
IPC: sbx-ipc (UNIX domain socket · SCM_RIGHTS handle passing · sbx-shm shared memory) | spawn + sandbox
      |              |               |                |                |
======|==============|===============|================|================|=========  TRUST BOUNDARY
      v              v               v                v                v
+-------------+ +-----------+ +-------------+ +-------------+
| Renderer    | | GPU       | | Network     | | Utility     |     (sandboxed children)
| (untrusted) | | process   | | process     | | process     |
| sbx-renderer| | relaxed   | | net caps    | | minimal caps|
| seccomp-bpf | | sandbox   | | sandbox     | | sandbox     |
| +namespaces | |           | |             | |             |
+-------------+ +-----------+ +-------------+ +-------------+
      \___ brokered file/socket/shm requests (validated vs CapabilitySet) ___/
sbx-sandbox (seccomp-bpf · user/pid/net/mnt/ipc/uts ns · pivot_root · no_new_privs · cap drop)
applies confinement to each child; a renderer compromise is contained to its CapabilitySet.

3.1 The three boundaries

Boundary Where Rule Consequence
Trust boundary (the line above) between browser/broker and every child child input is adversarial; the TCB validates everything; nothing the child says is trusted the single most important invariant in the doc — every OS access by a child is denied by seccomp OR brokered + validated vs CapabilitySet; defense in depth = TWO independent layers, a bug in one must not yield escape
Concurrency boundary OS process / address space each process = its own address space (no shared mutable memory except explicit sbx-shm regions); a child cannot reach into TCB memory browser runs an event loop + one IPC thread per child; children run their own loops; sole cross-process channels = sbx-ipc pipes + sbx-shm buffers + SCM_RIGHTS handle passes
Persistence boundary runtime state vs disk no durable runtime state required; the process graph + route table + CapabilitySets live in TCB memory and are rebuilt on restart only sbx-crash writes to disk (Minidump-style artifacts + crash log); a renderer cannot persist anything outside its brokered caps

Trust boundary detail (the load-bearing line). Threat = a renderer whose memory is fully controlled by an attacker (arbitrary code execution inside the renderer is assumed, not a failure). Containment, not prevention, is the goal. A compromised renderer holds exactly its CapabilitySet; it cannot widen it because (1) seccomp-bpf blocks the syscalls that would open new resources directly, and (2) every brokered request is re-validated by the PolicyEngine against the recorded CapabilitySet for that ProcessId — the renderer's own claims are ignored. Escape requires defeating both layers simultaneously.

Concurrency model. Browser = single-threaded event loop owning the ProcessHost registry + router, plus a dedicated blocking IPC reader thread per child (each thread owns one MessagePipe, demultiplexes by RouteId, hands parsed Messages to the loop via a channel). No child shares an address space with the TCB or with another child. sbx-shm regions are the only shared-memory channel and are sealed (memfd seals) + mapped read-only on the consumer side wherever the producer is untrusted. SIGCHLD is handled by sbx-crash on a self-pipe to keep async-signal-safety.

Persistence model. Runtime requires zero on-disk state: the site→process map, route table, and per-process CapabilitySets are reconstructible. The only writers to disk are sbx-crash (Minidump artifact + structured crash report) and observability sinks (metrics/traces in Phase 12). Children write nothing to disk except through a FileBroker grant.

3.2 The boxes (one per crate)

sbx-core — OS-free shared vocabulary. Defines ProcessId, ProcessType (Browser/Renderer/Gpu/Network/Utility/Storage), ProcessRole (Privileged for the browser, Sandboxed for children), SiteId, BrowsingInstanceId, HandleId, RouteId, Capability/CapabilitySet, SbxError. Inputs: none (pure types). Outputs: the type alphabet every other crate speaks. Security invariant: CapabilitySet is the only representation of a principal's authority — least-privilege grants are expressed here as Capability variants (OpenFile{path_glob,mode}, ConnectSocket{host,port}, SharedMemory{max_bytes}, …), nothing implicit. Referenced by every phase; the capability model is fleshed out in Phase 8 (## 20).

sbx-ipc — the Mojo-like typed IPC layer over UNIX domain sockets. Defines MessagePipe (bidirectional UDS pair via pair() → socketpair), Message{MessageHeader{route,kind,req_id,num_handles}, payload, handles}, Handle (owned OS fd), Interface/Endpoint<T> typed binding, request/response + async. Inputs: a connected UDS + typed interface contracts. Outputs: framed messages + duplicated fds. Security invariant: the TCB never trusts wire bytes — every inbound Message is bounds-checked, num_handles is reconciled with the actual SCM_RIGHTS ancillary data, and a malformed frame returns an error rather than panicking. Built in Phase 1 (## 13, framing/typed binding) and extended in Phase 2 (## 14, handle passing); fuzzed in Phase 13 (## 25).

sbx-shm — shared memory for bulk/zero-copy transfer. Defines SharedBuffer (memfd-backed, carries a Handle), Mapping (mmap region, Prot::ReadOnly/ReadWrite), ShmRing (SPSC ring over a SharedBuffer), seal flags. Inputs: a size + protection request. Outputs: a sealed memfd Handle passable over sbx-ipc. Security invariant: buffers from an untrusted producer are mapped read-only on the TCB side and sealed (F_SEAL_SHRINK|GROW|WRITE as applicable) so a renderer cannot shrink/grow/race a region the broker is reading — no TOCTOU on shared length. Used wherever IPC payloads exceed the small-message path; introduced in Phase 2 (## 14).

sbx-sandbox — the OS confinement engine and the heart of containment. Defines the Sandbox trait + LinuxSandbox (primary), SeccompFilter (compiles SyscallRule[] → a classic-BPF program over seccomp_data), SyscallPolicy with Disposition (Allow/Errno/Trap/KillProcess/Log) + arg filters, Namespaces (USER|PID|NET|MNT|IPC|UTS clone flags), Policy{namespaces, seccomp, rlimits, root (pivot_root target), drop_caps, no_new_privs}. Inputs: a Policy per ProcessType. Outputs: a confined process. Security invariants: (1) the BPF program checks arch first, rejecting anything but the expected AUDIT_ARCH_* to block the x32/ia32 ABI bypass; (2) the filter is installed only after PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS; (3) deny-by-default — unknown syscalls map to Errno/KillProcess, never Allow. macOS (Seatbelt) + Windows (job/restricted-token/AppContainer) backends are PhaseDeferred stubs. Built across Phase 4 (## 16, namespaces + fs) and Phase 5 (## 17, seccomp-bpf).

sbx-zygote — the pre-forked template for fast renderer spawn. Defines Zygote (fork-server socket + base sandbox policy), ForkRequest{process_type, policy, channel}, ForkServer (services requests by fork() + seal). Inputs: a ForkRequest over its control socket. Outputs: a new sandboxed child sharing COW pages with the template. Security invariant: the zygote pre-applies the common sandbox before any untrusted work, and each forked child seals/tightens its seccomp policy to the per-ProcessType profile before running renderer code — the template never executes untrusted input, and a forked child cannot weaken the filter (seccomp is monotonic). Built in Phase 6 (## 18); consumed by sbx-launcher via use_zygote.

sbx-broker — the capability-validating resource gateway. Defines FileBroker/SocketBroker/ResourceBroker, the Broker trait, BrokerRequest{from, op}, BrokerResponse (Handle/Denied/Error), and the PolicyEngine (maps ProcessId → CapabilitySet, validates each BrokerOp). Inputs: a BrokerRequest arriving over sbx-ipc from a child that cannot perform the operation itself (NET namespace removes sockets; pivot_root + seccomp remove file opens). Outputs: a brokered fd returned via SCM_RIGHTS, or a denial. Security invariant: deny-by-default + the broker opens, never the renderer — the renderer sends a request, the broker validates it against the recorded CapabilitySet (path glob, host/port, byte cap), opens the resource itself, and passes only the resulting fd; a renderer never gains the ability to name resources outside its grant. This is layer two of defense in depth; built in Phase 7 (## 19), policy profiles in Phase 8 (## 20).

sbx-launcher — process spawning + lifecycle. Defines LaunchOptions{process_type, policy, use_zygote}, ChildProcess{pid, channel}, ProcessLauncher, ProcessHandle. Inputs: LaunchOptions. Outputs: a running, sandboxed child with a connected MessagePipe and a completed handshake. Responsibilities: build the child command/args/env, create the IPC channel, route through the zygote or do a direct clone/exec, run the handshake, then monitor/reap via SIGCHLD with restart hooks. Security invariant: the IPC channel + sandbox Policy are wired before the child runs untrusted work — there is no window in which a child is alive but unconfined. Built in Phase 3 (## 15, no sandbox yet), then composed with sbx-sandbox + sbx-zygote in Phases 4–6.

sbx-site — site-isolation policy. Defines SiteInstance{id, site, browsing_instance}, BrowsingInstance, ProcessReusePolicy (ProcessPerSiteInstance / ReuseExistingForSite / LimitReached), SiteIsolationPolicy (eTLD+1 derivation via public-suffix list, site_for(url), site→process map). Inputs: a navigation URL. Outputs: a SiteId and a process-assignment decision. Security invariant: the principal is the SiteInstance — site = scheme + registrable domain (eTLD+1); cross-site documents land in separate processes, so a renderer compromise cannot read another site's data even via Spectre-class side channels. SiteInstances within one BrowsingInstance may script each other and so share a process. Built in Phase 9 (## 21); consumed by the orchestrator's assignment logic.

sbx-browser — the orchestrator and TCB entry point. Defines BrowserProcess (owns ProcessHost, launcher, brokers, site policy, crash handler, router), ProcessHost (registry of all child hosts keyed by ProcessId), RenderProcessHost{pid, site_instance, routes}. Inputs: navigations + inbound child IPC. Outputs: process spawn/assignment decisions + routed messages. Responsibilities: map navigation → SiteInstance → process (new or reused per ProcessReusePolicy), multiplex many frames over one pipe via RouteId, own every child's lifetime. Security invariant: it is the sole privileged actor — all broker validation, site assignment, and routing happen here; a RouteId is bound to one RenderProcessHost, so a child cannot inject messages on a route it does not own. Wired end-to-end in Phase 10 (## 22).

sbx-renderer — the minimal untrusted child (a stand-in, not a web engine; real rendering is #260/#305). Defines RendererProcess (sandboxed; talks to broker; hosts RenderFrames; has a test crash hook) and RenderFrame{route, url}. Inputs: a navigation + brokered resources. Outputs: broker requests + IPC responses; deliberately, nothing it isn't granted. Security invariant: it runs fully sealed (seccomp + all namespaces + pivot_root + dropped caps + no_new_privs) and can reach the OS only through the broker — it is the device under test for the Phase 13 (## 25) red-team harness, which forces it to attempt forbidden syscalls/files/sockets and asserts each is denied or kills it.

sbx-crash — crash handling + recovery. Defines CrashHandler (SIGCHLD/exit/IPC-disconnect watcher → Minidump + RestartPolicy + sad-tab signal), Minidump (minimal artifact: pid, signal, register-ish snapshot, maps), RestartPolicy (Never / Restart{max,window_secs} / ReloadTab). Inputs: child-death signals. Outputs: a crash artifact + a recovery action. Security invariant: blast radius is one child — a renderer crash is observed (never trusted to self-report cleanly) and isolated; the browser and sibling renderers are unaffected and the user sees a "sad tab." Crash artifact writes are the only runtime disk persistence. Built in Phase 11 (## 23).

sbx-cli — the sbx operator binary. Subcommands: run, spawn, policy, ipc-ping, sandbox-test, crash-test, sites, attack (the red-team harness). Inputs: operator commands. Outputs: an end-to-end driver of the whole stack for demos, conformance, and the security gate. Security note: attack is the executable form of the trust boundary — it must prove that forbidden operations are denied/killed, anchoring the Phase 13 (## 25) "done" gate. Depends on all crates above.

4. System Constraints

Hard, measurable bounds on the process model + sandbox. All targets: Linux x86-64 primary backend, release build, on a 2-core warm machine, kernel >= 5.10 (seccomp KILL_PROCESS, memfd seals, unprivileged userns assumed enabled). Targets are SLOs; the security invariants (C-SEC-*) are NON-negotiable and gate the build.

4.1 Trust boundary (the overriding constraint)

  • The browser/broker process is the TCB; everything across the TRUST BOUNDARY (renderer/GPU/network/utility) is fully untrusted, possibly attacker-controlled.
  • C-SEC-1 — never trust child input. Every Message, BrokerRequest, Handle, length, offset, and FD count arriving from a child is adversarial: bounds-checked, type-validated, capability-checked before any privileged action. No path/string/index from a child is dereferenced unvalidated.
  • C-SEC-2 — defense in depth = two independent layers. A renderer's OS access is DENIED by seccomp and brokered+validated against its CapabilitySet. A single layer's bug must not yield escape.
  • C-SEC-3 — deny by default. Unlisted syscall → Errno/Kill; unlisted resource → Denied. New capability requires explicit grant.
  • C-SEC-4 — zero ambient privilege in a sandboxed child (see 4.4).
  • C-SEC-5 — blast radius bounded by CapabilitySet. A compromised renderer gains only its own grants; broker re-validation prevents escalation across the boundary.

4.2 Quantified performance + resource targets

# Constraint Target Hard ceiling Measured by (Phase)
C-1 Renderer spawn — warm zygote fork+seal p50 < 10 ms p99 < 30 ms bench (P6/P13)
C-2 Renderer spawn — cold clone+exec+sandbox < 60 ms < 100 ms bench (P3/P4/P13)
C-3 Sandbox apply (install namespaces + seccomp + pivot_root + caps) < 5 ms < 10 ms bench (P4/P5)
C-4 IPC round-trip, small Message (<= 256 B, no handle) p50 < 50 us p99 < 200 us bench (P1/P13)
C-5 IPC small-message throughput (1 pipe) > 200k msg/s — bench (P1/P13)
C-6 Bulk transfer via sbx-shm ShmRing (zero-copy) > 1 GB/s — bench (P2/P13)
C-7 FD/handle pass latency (SCM_RIGHTS round-trip) < 100 us < 300 us bench (P2)
C-8 Per-renderer base memory overhead (RSS, idle, post-seal) < 30 MB < 50 MB bench (P12)
C-9 Broker request validate+serve (cached CapabilitySet) p50 < 100 us p99 < 500 us bench (P7/P13)
C-10 Crash detect → "sad tab" + restart available < 200 ms < 500 ms test (P11)
C-11 Browser TCB resident overhead (no children) < 40 MB — bench (P12)
C-12 Site lookup site_for(url) (eTLD+1 via PSL) < 20 us < 100 us bench (P9)

4.3 Process-count + reuse constraints

  • C-13 — process ceiling. Hard cap MAX_RENDERER_PROCS (default 64, configurable; derived from RAM via total_ram / C-8 ceiling). At cap, ProcessReusePolicy transitions ProcessPerSiteInstance → ReuseExistingForSite → LimitReached.
  • C-14 — reuse threshold. Reuse kicks in at >= 80% of the cap; a new SiteInstance whose SiteId already has a host reuses it; same-BrowsingInstance script-related instances always coalesce to one process.
  • C-15 — process-per-site-instance is the invariant below threshold; reuse never merges two distinct SiteIds in different BrowsingInstances (would breach site isolation → forbidden).
  • C-16 — FD budget per process. Each child runs under RLIMIT_NOFILE <= 256 (renderer) / 1024 (network); the browser sizes its own fd table for MAX_RENDERER_PROCS x (1 pipe + brokered FDs) with headroom. FD exhaustion → graceful SpawnFailed, never browser abort.

4.4 Sandboxed-child zero-privilege invariants (Linux, post-seal)

Invariant Required end state
Capabilities bounding set empty; no_new_privs = 1; all 40+ caps dropped
Network NET namespace with no interfaces (loopback down) → all egress brokered
Filesystem pivot_root to minimal, mostly read-only rootfs; no host /, /proc masked/minimal
Namespaces user + pid + net + mnt + ipc + uts unshared; child sees itself as pid 1 in its pid ns
Syscalls seccomp-bpf installed; arch checked first (reject non-AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64 → blocks x32/ia32 ABI bypass); unlisted → Errno(ENOSYS)/KillProcess
Privilege UID/GID mapped to fake root inside userns only; zero real host privilege
Identity renderer CapabilitySet derived from its SiteInstance principal; default profile = minimal

4.5 Availability + blast-radius constraints

  • C-AV-1 — child death != browser death. A renderer crash (SIGSEGV, seccomp SIGSYS, OOM-kill, IPC disconnect) NEVER takes down the browser or any other renderer. Detected via SIGCHLD/exit/IPC-EOF; surfaced as "sad tab".
  • C-AV-2 — no shared mutable state across the boundary except explicitly via sbx-shm buffers with seals; a child cannot corrupt browser memory.
  • C-AV-3 — bounded restart. RestartPolicy::Restart { max, window_secs } (default max 3 / 60 s) caps restart storms; exceeding → Never/ReloadTab, no busy-loop.
  • C-AV-4 — malformed input is non-fatal. A malformed Message/BrokerRequest from a child yields SbxError/connection teardown, never a panic/UB in the TCB (enforced by IPC fuzzing, P13).

4.6 Platform, portability + scope constraints

  • Linux primary: hard dependency on seccomp-bpf, unprivileged user namespaces, pivot_root, memfd_create + seals, SCM_RIGHTS. Degradation: if unprivileged userns disabled (kernel.unprivileged_userns_clone=0), spawn fails closed with actionable error — never silently runs unsandboxed.
  • macOS (Seatbelt) / Windows (job object + restricted token / AppContainer): Sandbox backend stubs behind PhaseDeferred — must compile, must Err(SbxError::Sandbox) at apply, must not pretend to confine.
  • MSRV 1.78, edition 2021; unsafe confined to syscall wrappers (clone/seccomp/SCM_RIGHTS/mmap), each with a documented safety contract; no unsafe in policy/validation logic.
  • NON-GOALS (constraints by exclusion): no real layout/JS engine (renderer is a minimal untrusted stand-in); no network stack; not a general container runtime or hypervisor. The renderer must NOT acquire rendering/network/file capability beyond test fixtures.

4.7 Determinism + testability constraints

  • Sandbox policies + capability profiles are declarative + static per ProcessType → reproducible, auditable, snapshot-testable.
  • Spawn/IPC/broker paths expose injectable clocks + counters → all C-1..C-12 targets are CI-asserted regressions, not aspirations.
  • The red-team harness (sbx attack, P13) is a release gate: every forbidden syscall/file/socket attempt MUST be denied or kill the child; a single bypass fails the build.

5. Required Reading

Curated, prioritized. Read TCB-critical sources (seccomp, namespaces, SCM_RIGHTS, capabilities) before writing sbx-sandbox/sbx-broker. Each entry: what to extract, what NOT to copy. "Reference Implementations" are studied for design, never vendored — we re-derive in safe Rust (sbx-*), eliminating the C++ memory-unsafety class.
