General rule: study architecture and policy content; transcribe no C/C++. Reference impls inform the what (which syscalls to allow, fork protocol shape); sbx-* owns the how in safe Rust.

5.4 Specs / Man Pages (normative — read before implementing the cited phase)

Spec Phase Must-internalize
seccomp(2) + SECCOMP_SET_MODE_FILTER 5 (## 17) Filter install requires no_new_privs OR CAP_SYS_ADMIN; filters stack; cannot be removed; SECCOMP_FILTER_FLAG_TSYNC
struct seccomp_data (in seccomp(2)) 5 Layout { int nr; __u32 arch; __u64 instruction_pointer; __u64 args[6]; }; cBPF reads big offsets into this; check arch first (AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64) to block x32/ia32 ABI bypass; args are zero/sign-extension caveats for 32-bit compares
bpf(2) / classic-BPF (linux/filter.h) 5 cBPF instruction set (BPF_LD/BPF_JMP/BPF_RET); no loops; program-length limit; SECCOMP_RET_* action precedence (lowest action wins when filters stack)
user_namespaces(7) 4 (## 16) Unprivileged user-ns gives fake root → enables other namespaces without real privilege; uid_map/gid_map write rules; setgroups=deny required before gid_map; /proc/sys/kernel/unprivileged_userns_clone gate
pid_namespaces(7), network_namespaces(7), mount_namespaces(7), namespaces(7) 4 PID-ns hides processes (PID 1 reaping); empty NET-ns removes all networking → forces brokering; MNT-ns + propagation flags (MS_PRIVATE)
pivot_root(2) 4 Constraints: new_root must be a mount, not the current root; sequence mount --bind → pivot_root → umount2(old, MNT_DETACH); preferred over chroot (which is escapable)
clone(2) 3 (## 15), 4, 6 CLONE_NEW* flags (`USER
unix(7) + cmsg(3) + SCM_RIGHTS 1–2 (## 13–14) AF_UNIX SOCK_SEQPACKET/SOCK_STREAM; SCM_RIGHTS duplicates FDs into receiver; MSG_CMSG_CLOEXEC to avoid leak race; recvmsg truncation → MSG_CTRUNC; max FDs per message
socketpair(2) 1 Connected AF_UNIX pair backing sbx-ipc::pair() / MessagePipe
memfd_create(2) + fcntl(2) F_ADD_SEALS 2 (## 14) Anonymous file → SharedBuffer; seals F_SEAL_SHRINK/F_SEAL_GROW/F_SEAL_WRITE/F_SEAL_SEAL; F_SEAL_WRITE requires no existing writable mappings; sealing enables safe RO sharing to untrusted peer
mmap(2) / mprotect(2) 2 MAP_SHARED over the memfd FD → Mapping; Prot::ReadOnly enforced via PROT_READ even if sender retains RW
prctl(2) PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS 4–5 Set once, irreversible; prerequisite for unprivileged seccomp install; also blocks SUID/file-cap privilege gain
capabilities(7) 4 The five sets; drop bounding set + clear ambient + empty inheritable → child can never regain privilege; combine with no_new_privs
getrlimit(2)/setrlimit(2), prlimit(2) 4, 12 (## 24) RLIMIT_AS/NOFILE/NPROC/CPU/CORE(=0 to suppress core dumps from untrusted child) → Rlimits
signalfd(2) / sigaction(2) (SIGCHLD, SIGSYS) 5, 11 (## 23) SIGSYS delivered on SECCOMP_RET_TRAP (siginfo si_syscall/si_arch); SIGCHLD+signalfd for race-free reaping in CrashHandler
Public Suffix List (publicsuffix.org spec) + https://url.spec.whatwg.org 9 (## 21) eTLD+1 / registrable-domain algorithm (ICANN vs PRIVATE sections, wildcard * + exception ! rules); URL parsing for scheme/host → SiteIsolationPolicy::site_for
proc(5) (/proc/<pid>/status, smaps_rollup, stat) 12 Per-process memory/CPU sampling for budgets + metrics; PSS via smaps_rollup

Reading order for first implementation pass: B1 → P3/P4 → seccomp(2)+seccomp_data+user_namespaces(7)+pivot_root(2)+clone(2) → unix(7)/SCM_RIGHTS/memfd_create(2) → P1/P2 + Public Suffix List → P9/P11 (threat-model framing) → R1/R4/R7/R8 for policy content.

6. Design Principles

Non-negotiable invariants. Every later phase MUST cite the principle(s) it upholds; any PR that violates one is rejected at review regardless of test status. Numbered P1..P14 for reference.

6.1 P1 — The renderer is fully untrusted (assume RCE)

  • Assume every renderer is already attacker-controlled. Treat the renderer binary as if a remote attacker has arbitrary code execution inside it from the first instruction of untrusted work.
  • Rationale: a real renderer parses adversarial input (HTML/JS/images/fonts); historically the dominant CVE source. We model that endpoint as hostile by construction, not "potentially buggy".
  • Tradeoff: forbids all "the renderer already validated it" shortcuts; every cross-boundary datum is re-checked in the TCB → higher IPC + broker cost.
  • Operational implication: no renderer-supplied value is ever trusted as authority — not a path, host, FD number, length, route, or capability claim. The renderer states intent; the browser decides.
  • Violation example: FileBroker opens req.path because "the renderer is ours" → compromised renderer requests /etc/shadow, escapes its CapabilitySet. Breaks P2, P6, P10.

6.2 P2 — Deny by default

  • The sandbox and broker permit nothing that is not explicitly granted. SeccompFilter baseline = KillProcess/Errno for all syscalls except an enumerated allow-list; PolicyEngine returns Denied unless a matching Capability is found.
  • Rationale: allow-lists fail safe; new syscalls/features default to forbidden, not exposed.
  • Tradeoff: every needed syscall/op must be discovered + justified + added explicitly → upfront enumeration cost, churn when the renderer's needs change.
  • Operational implication: policy reviews are about what was added, never what was forgotten. Default arm of every match in SyscallPolicy and PolicyEngine is the deny disposition.
  • Violation example: seccomp default disposition set to Allow "to unblock dev" → a forgotten dangerous syscall (ptrace, process_vm_writev, clone(CLONE_NEWUSER)) is reachable. Breaks P3.

6.3 P3 — Defense in depth (seccomp AND broker, never one layer)

  • Two independent layers gate every OS resource: the seccomp syscall filter AND the broker policy. A single layer's bug must not yield escape.
  • Mechanism: NET namespace removes sockets and SocketBroker validates ConnectSocket; pivot_root limits the FS and FileBroker validates OpenFile. seccomp blocks the raw syscall; the broker is the only sanctioned path, itself capability-checked.
  • Rationale: layers have uncorrelated failure modes — a seccomp arg-filter gap is not also a PolicyEngine gap.
  • Tradeoff: duplicated enforcement, two policies to keep consistent, two test suites.
  • Operational implication: every resource has a named layer-1 (seccomp/namespace) AND layer-2 (broker/capability) control; phase docs list both.
  • Violation example: relying solely on NET namespace, no SocketBroker check → a future GPU/network process that does hold a socket FD becomes a confused deputy. Breaks P4, P11.

6.4 P4 — Least privilege per process type

  • Each ProcessType runs with the minimal CapabilitySet for its job; only the browser holds real OS privilege. Renderer = minimal (no net, no fs beyond rootfs, brokered everything). Network/GPU = relaxed only in their specific axis. Utility = near-empty.
  • Per-type default profiles defined in Phase 8 (## 20); principal of authority = SiteInstance.
  • Tradeoff: more process types + per-type policy matrices to maintain vs one uniform sandbox.
  • Operational implication: a new capability is granted to exactly one process type, justified against that type's role; no "grant to all children" shortcut.
  • Violation example: GPU process given the renderer's relaxed-plus-net profile "for convenience" → widens blast radius of a GPU compromise to the network. Breaks P10.

6.5 P5 — The browser is the TCB: memory-safe and minimal

  • The browser/broker is the Trusted Computing Base; it is written memory-safe (Rust, no unsafe outside audited syscall shims) and kept small. Every byte arriving from a child is untrusted input parsed inside the TCB.
  • Rationale: the entire language choice (vs C/C++) exists to remove the memory-unsafety attack surface in the privilege-separation code that sits on the trust boundary.
  • Tradeoff: unsafe is sometimes required (clone/seccomp/mmap/SCM_RIGHTS) → it is quarantined, minimized, audited, and never fed unvalidated child input.
  • Operational implication: TCB LOC is a tracked metric; new TCB code needs justification; child-derived bytes are validated before any unsafe.
  • Violation example: broker copies a child-supplied length into an unsafe slice without bounds check → OOB read/write in the TCB = full escape. Breaks P1, P13.

6.6 P6 — Brokered, not direct

  • No path, hostname, or port crosses the trust boundary as an authority — only validated OS handles via SCM_RIGHTS. The broker opens the resource and passes back only the FD (BrokerResponse::Handle); the renderer never names a resource the kernel will act on directly.
  • Mechanism: SCM_RIGHTS duplicates an FD into the child = the capability-passing primitive. The string the renderer sends is intent, validated against its CapabilitySet before any open.
  • Tradeoff: extra IPC round-trip + broker CPU per resource access vs direct syscalls.
  • Operational implication: a child holds handles, not names; revoking a capability means refusing future broker requests, not racing to close strings.
  • Violation example: broker returns the resolved path and lets the child open() it → TOCTOU + the child can mutate the path; the capability check is meaningless. Breaks P1, P3.

6.7 P7 — Capabilities are the unit of authority

  • All authority is expressed as Capability values held in a per-process CapabilitySet; there is no ambient authority. OpenFile{path_glob,mode}, ConnectSocket{host,port}, SharedMemory{max_bytes}, etc. The PolicyEngine maps ProcessId -> CapabilitySet and checks each BrokerOp against it.
  • Rationale: authority is explicit, enumerable, diff-able, and bounded; "what can this PID do?" has a single, complete answer.
  • Tradeoff: every grant must be modeled as a Capability; ad-hoc permissions are disallowed → some friction.
  • Operational implication: audit = dump the CapabilitySet per ProcessId; grant/revoke is a set mutation; principal = SiteInstance (Phase 8).
  • Violation example: broker checks a global flag instead of the caller's set → one over-privileged renderer leaks authority to all. Breaks P4, P10.

6.8 P8 — Site isolation is a security boundary, not an optimization

  • SiteInstance (scheme + eTLD+1) is a hard security boundary: distinct sites get distinct processes so that cross-site data is separated by OS-process isolation, not in-process logic. It is NOT merely a perf/parallelism feature.
  • Mechanism: process-per-site-instance keyed on the public-suffix-list registrable domain; SiteInstances in one BrowsingInstance may script each other so may share a process; cross-BrowsingInstance never shares.
  • Tradeoff: more processes → more memory + spawn cost; bounded by a process-count limit + ProcessReusePolicy (Phase 9), but reuse must never merge mutually-distrusting sites.
  • Operational implication: process-count pressure relaxes via reuse within a site, never across sites; the limit degrades performance, never the boundary.
  • Violation example: under memory pressure two different eTLD+1 sites share a renderer → a renderer RCE reads the other site's secrets in-process. Breaks P1, P10.

6.9 P9 — Fail closed

  • Any failure to fully apply confinement kills the child; a process never runs unconfined. Sandbox::apply returning Err → the child is terminated before executing untrusted work, surfaced as SbxError::Sandbox/SpawnFailed.
  • Scope: namespace creation, pivot_root, cap-drop, PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS, seccomp install, broker channel handshake — partial success is failure.
  • Rationale: a half-sandboxed renderer is more dangerous than no renderer; the safe error state is "dead", never "running with reduced protection".
  • Tradeoff: transient host conditions (rlimit, no userns) cause hard launch failures rather than degraded operation.
  • Operational implication: ordering matters — seccomp is installed after no_new_privs; the child blocks on a sealed-confinement signal before touching untrusted input.
  • Violation example: seccomp install fails, code logs + continues → renderer runs with full syscall surface. Breaks P2, P3.

6.10 P10 — Blast radius is bounded by design

  • A full compromise of a child yields exactly that child's CapabilitySet and nothing more. Because the broker re-validates every request against the set, a compromised renderer cannot escalate; it can only do what it was already authorized to do.
  • Rationale: security is reasoned about as containment, not prevention — we assume the inner perimeter falls (P1) and bound the consequences.
  • Tradeoff: requires the capability model (P7) + site isolation (P8) + brokering (P6) to all hold simultaneously; weakening any one widens the radius.
  • Operational implication: threat model (## 26) quantifies blast radius per process type as exactly its profile; red-team harness (Phase 13) proves nothing outside the set is reachable.
  • Violation example: broker caches a previously-validated FD and serves it to a now-different request/PID → authority leaks beyond the original set. Breaks P3, P7.

6.11 P11 — Crash isolation (renderer death != browser death)

  • A child crash must never crash or corrupt the browser; it is detected, contained, and surfaced as a "sad tab". CrashHandler observes child death via SIGCHLD/exit/IPC-disconnect, captures a Minidump, applies RestartPolicy, signals sad-tab; the browser + sibling renderers continue.
  • Rationale: process isolation is only valuable if a single process failure is locally contained — otherwise multi-process buys nothing.
  • Tradeoff: per-child supervision + reaping + restart bookkeeping; restart storms bounded by RestartPolicy::Restart{max,window_secs}.
  • Operational implication: all child-facing browser code is panic-isolated and treats Ipc/ChildCrashed errors as routine, never as a reason to abort the browser.
  • Violation example: the IPC router unwraps a disconnect error → browser panics, all tabs die from one renderer crash. Breaks the entire multi-process value proposition.

6.12 P12 — Validate every IPC message

  • Every Message is validated before use: declared payload length vs received bytes, num_handles vs actually-received FD count, route against known RouteIds, req_id correlation, bounds on every offset. Never trust an offset, length, or handle count from the wire.
  • Rationale: the wire is an attacker-controlled channel from an untrusted child (P1); deserialization is the classic memory-corruption entry into a TCB.
  • Tradeoff: per-message validation cost; strict caps on payload size + handle count reject oversized messages rather than allocating to them.
  • Operational implication: malformed/oversized/handle-count-mismatched messages → drop + log + treat as child misbehavior (close pipe), never crash; IPC fuzzing (Phase 13) is a gate.
  • Violation example: code mmaps using a child-supplied SharedBuffer.len without sanity bound → OOM or OOB in the broker. Breaks P5.

6.13 P13 — Reproducible, auditable policy

  • The seccomp and capability policies are data, not scattered code: declared as SyscallRule[] / CapabilitySet profiles that are reviewable, diff-able, and independently tested. A reviewer reads the policy table, not the enforcement plumbing.
  • Mechanism: SyscallPolicy DSL → SeccompFilter cBPF; per-ProcessType profiles are named, versioned data; conformance tests assert each rule's disposition.
  • Tradeoff: a DSL/data layer to build + maintain vs hand-coding filters inline.
  • Operational implication: policy changes are small, legible diffs with a test per rule; the arch check (reject non-AUDIT_ARCH_*, block x32/ia32 bypass) is an asserted invariant, not an assumption.
  • Violation example: filter built imperatively in 300 lines of branching → a reviewer cannot tell which syscalls are allowed; a regression silently widens the allow-list. Breaks P2, P3.

6.14 P14 — No implicit trust transitivity

  • Trust does not flow through a child. A handle/capability the broker grants to renderer A is not usable by renderer B; a SiteInstance principal cannot act for another. Each BrokerRequest is authorized against the requesting ProcessId's current set, freshly.
  • Rationale: prevents confused-deputy + capability-leak attacks where one child relays authority to another via IPC.
  • Tradeoff: no cross-child fast paths; shared resources are modeled as explicit per-recipient grants (e.g. a ShmRing shared only between parties each holding the capability).
  • Operational implication: handles passed between children must route through the broker, which re-validates; the browser never forwards a child's authority on its behalf.
  • Violation example: renderer A SCM_RIGHTS-passes a brokered FD to renderer B over a side channel and B uses it beyond its own set → silent escalation. Mitigated because B's own CapabilitySet still gates every broker op; direct FD relay is the residual risk tracked in the threat model.

Cross-reference. Later phases cite these by tag: Phase 4–5 (## 16–## 17) realize P2/P3/P9/P13; Phase 7–8 (## 19–## 20) realize P4/P6/P7/P14; Phase 9 (## 21) realizes P8; Phase 11 (## 23) realizes P11; Phase 13 (## 25) red-team harness empirically validates P1/P2/P10/P12. P5 is a standing constraint on all TCB code in every phase.

7. System Invariants

The properties below MUST hold at all times. Each is stated as an assertion the system never violates, with: Why it matters, Enforced (crate/code path), Detection (compile-time / debug-assert / runtime check / test), Telemetry (metric + trace span), Recovery (fail-closed action), Debug (methodology when violated). Severity: CRIT-TB = trust-boundary, a breach is a sandbox escape or privilege escalation (highest); HIGH = isolation / safety; MED = correctness / liveness.

Numbering: INV-n. Trust-boundary invariants (INV-1..6, INV-9, INV-10) are CRIT-TB — a single violation is treated as a P0 security incident, not a bug.

7.1 Invariant summary table

ID Severity Statement (never-violate) Primary enforcer
INV-1 CRIT-TB Sandboxed child has EMPTY ambient authority before untrusted work sbx-sandbox, sbx-zygote
INV-2 CRIT-TB seccomp installed only after no_new_privs; arch checked first sbx-sandbox::SeccompFilter
INV-3 CRIT-TB Browser acts on NO un-validated child input (every Message + BrokerRequest) sbx-ipc, sbx-browser, sbx-broker
INV-4 CRIT-TB Every brokered resource validated vs caller CapabilitySet; deny by default sbx-broker::PolicyEngine
INV-5 CRIT-TB Sandbox-apply failure is fatal to the child (fail closed) sbx-launcher, sbx-zygote
INV-6 CRIT-TB Handles pass ONLY via SCM_RIGHTS; ownership single + tracked sbx-ipc::Handle
INV-7 HIGH Renderer crash cannot crash browser or sibling renderer sbx-crash, sbx-launcher
INV-8 HIGH Cross-site isolated docs NEVER co-located in one process sbx-site, sbx-browser
INV-9 CRIT-TB IPC framing bounds-checked (no OOB from length/handle count) sbx-ipc wire decoder
INV-10 CRIT-TB Zygote-forked child seals sandbox before touching untrusted data sbx-zygote::ForkServer
INV-11 HIGH Child PID/Handle in ProcessHost ⇔ live tracked entry (no orphan/dangling) sbx-browser::ProcessHost
INV-12 MED req_id uniqueness per pipe; no response to unknown/closed request sbx-ipc
INV-13 HIGH A renderer never holds a path-based FD it could open() itself sbx-broker, sbx-sandbox
INV-14 HIGH Two independent layers (seccomp AND broker) each fail-safe alone sbx-sandbox + sbx-broker

7.2 Trust-boundary invariants (CRIT-TB)

INV-1 — Empty ambient authority before untrusted work CRIT-TB

  • Statement: at the instant a child begins processing untrusted data, it has: 0 capabilities (CAP_* all dropped), no network (empty NET namespace, loopback only/down), minimal pivot_root rootfs (read-only, no host /proc view, no device nodes beyond an allowlist), no_new_privs=1, rlimits set, all inherited FDs except the IPC channel closed.
  • Why: ambient authority is the escape surface; a compromised renderer must start from zero — only its CapabilitySet (mediated by the broker) grants anything.
  • Enforced: sbx-sandbox::LinuxSandbox::apply(&Policy) runs the full sequence (namespaces → pivot_root → rlimits → no_new_privs → cap-drop → seccomp) before exec/before the zygote child returns to renderer code; sbx-launcher closes non-channel FDs (CLOEXEC + explicit /proc/self/fd sweep).
  • Detection: debug-build post-apply self-check reads /proc/self/status (CapEff==0, NoNewPrivs==1, Seccomp==2), confirms /proc/self/ns/* differs from parent, route probe confirms no egress. RED-TEAM sbx attack asserts each at runtime. CI gate.
  • Telemetry: metric sandbox_apply_verified_total{type}; span sandbox.apply attrs caps_dropped, ns_mask, rootfs, nnp, seccomp_mode.
  • Recovery: any self-check mismatch ⇒ abort() the child immediately (fail closed, INV-5). Never downgrade to a weaker policy.
  • Debug: capture /proc/<pid>/status + mountinfo + Seccomp line; diff vs expected Policy; replay sbx sandbox-test --type renderer --dump.

INV-2 — seccomp after no_new_privs, arch checked first CRIT-TB

  • Statement: the cBPF program (a) installs only after prctl(PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS,1) succeeds, and (b) its FIRST instructions load seccomp_data.arch and KILL any value != AUDIT_ARCH_<expected> (e.g. AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64), before any nr comparison.
  • Why: without no_new_privs an unprivileged process can't install a filter (and a setuid exec could regain privs); without the arch gate an attacker issues the same syscall number under the x32/ia32 ABI to reach an unfiltered number → classic seccomp bypass.
  • Enforced: SeccompFilter::compile() emits the arch-check prologue unconditionally; LinuxSandbox::apply orders no_new_privs strictly before seccomp(SECCOMP_SET_MODE_FILTER, …). Ordering lives in one function — not call-site discretion.
  • Detection: unit test asserts compiled BPF first 3 insns == [LD arch, JEQ expected, RET KILL]; integration test issues an x32 syscall (__X32_SYSCALL_BIT) → expects SIGSYS/kill. debug_assert!(nnp_set) guards the seccomp() call.
  • Telemetry: seccomp_install_total{arch_guard=true}, seccomp_arch_reject_total; span seccomp.install{prog_len, nnp_before}.
  • Recovery: if no_new_privs set fails → apply returns SbxError::Sandbox → child aborts (INV-5). Filter compile-invariant failure is a build-time panic.
  • Debug: dump BPF via seccomp-tools-style disassembler in sbx sandbox-test --dump-bpf; confirm prologue; check dmesg/audit for the SECCOMP kill with offending nr|arch.

INV-3 — No action on un-validated child input CRIT-TB

  • Statement: the browser/broker performs NO privileged effect (open file, connect, map shm, route, spawn) from child-supplied bytes until those bytes parse into a typed value AND that value passes validation. Raw payload/Handle from a child is data, never a trusted command.
  • Why: the child is fully hostile; treating its bytes as authoritative is the canonical confused-deputy escape.
  • Enforced: sbx-ipc decodes wire bytes → typed Message/Interface request (bounds-checked, INV-9); sbx-browser router dispatches only typed, route-validated messages; sbx-broker::PolicyEngine::validate precedes every BrokerOp effect; no API takes a raw renderer path/host/length straight into a syscall.
  • Detection: type system (a BrokerOp cannot be built without the decoder); #[must_use] on validation results; fuzz harness feeds malformed Messages (INV-9) asserting no effect/no crash; review checklist: every TCB syscall traces to a validated value.
  • Telemetry: ipc_msg_decoded_total{kind}, ipc_msg_rejected_total{reason}, broker_validate_total{op,verdict}; spans broker.request{from_pid, op, verdict}, ipc.dispatch{route, kind}.
  • Recovery: decode/validate failure → drop the message, bump reject metric, optionally Denied/Error response; repeated violations from one child → flag for kill (INV-7 path). Never "best-effort" the request.
  • Debug: log the raw frame (hex, length-capped) + decoder verdict + from PID + CapabilitySet; reproduce via sbx ipc-ping --raw <frame>.

INV-4 — Brokered resource validated vs CapabilitySet; deny by default CRIT-TB

  • Statement: for every BrokerRequest{from, op}, PolicyEngine looks up from's CapabilitySet and returns a Handle ONLY if some held Capability explicitly authorizes op (path glob + AccessMode for OpenFile; host+port for ConnectSocket; max_bytes for SharedMemory). The default verdict — including unknown from or unmatched op — is Denied.
  • Why: the broker is the only path to OS resources a sandboxed child lacks; granting without proof of capability collapses least privilege.
  • Enforced: sbx-broker::PolicyEngine holds ProcessId → CapabilitySet (populated at spawn from the per-ProcessType default profile + site-scoped grants); Broker::service calls validate first, returns BrokerResponse::Denied on any miss; capability match is allowlist (OpenFile canonicalizes path vs glob, rejecting ../symlink traversal).
  • Detection: unit tests per Capability variant (positive + negative: glob edge cases, symlink escape, port off-by-one); RED-TEAM sbx attack --broker requests un-granted files/hosts → asserts Denied; property test: random op with empty CapabilitySet ⇒ always Denied.
  • Telemetry: broker_request_total{op}, broker_denied_total{op,reason}, broker_grant_total{op}; span broker.policy{from, op, matched_cap?, verdict}.
  • Recovery: deny is the safe path (no FD returned). An over-broad grant later ⇒ revoke + audit. A map-lookup miss for a live PID is itself a bug → deny + alert.
  • Debug: dump from's CapabilitySet, the op, and the matching-predicate trace; sbx policy --pid <id> prints the effective set; replay the exact BrokerOp.

INV-5 — Sandbox-apply failure is fatal to the child (fail closed) CRIT-TB

  • Statement: if ANY step of Sandbox::apply (namespace create, pivot_root, rlimit, no_new_privs, cap-drop, seccomp install) fails, the child MUST terminate before executing one instruction of untrusted work. There is no "partially sandboxed" or "unconfined fallback" path.
  • Why: running untrusted code with a half-applied or absent sandbox is strictly worse than not running it — it is an open escape.
  • Enforced: apply returns Result<(),SbxError>; in sbx-launcher (direct clone/exec) and sbx-zygote::ForkServer (forked child) the child calls apply and on Err abort()/_exit(127) immediately, before exec/before returning to renderer logic. Parent observes non-launch → SbxError::SpawnFailed.
  • Detection: fault-injection build flag forces each step to fail → test asserts the child exits non-zero and never reaches the renderer-entry sentinel; debug_assert! that renderer-main is unreachable on the error branch.
  • Telemetry: sandbox_apply_fail_total{step}, child_fail_closed_total; span sandbox.apply ends status=failed, failed_step=….
  • Recovery: parent surfaces SpawnFailed to the navigation/host; retry per policy with a fresh child; never reuse the half-set-up child.
  • Debug: child writes failing step + errno to the pre-seal error pipe before exit; parent logs it; reproduce with sbx sandbox-test --inject-fail <step>.

INV-6 — Handles only via SCM_RIGHTS; single tracked ownership CRIT-TB

  • Statement: OS FDs cross the trust boundary ONLY as ancillary data (SCM_RIGHTS) on the IPC socket. Each received FD is owned by exactly one Handle; closing happens exactly once; no FD leaks to a child it wasn't granted; the sender's attached-FD count equals header.num_handles.
  • Why: FD passing IS capability passing — the broker hands a renderer an open FD, never a path (INV-13). A double-close corrupts the TCB FD table (use-after-close → wrong-FD writes); a leak grants unintended authority.
  • Enforced: sbx-ipc::Handle is a move-only RAII owner (no Clone; Drop closes once; into_raw/from_raw transfer ownership explicitly); the sender sets O_CLOEXEC on everything not deliberately passed; the wire decoder ties received FDs 1:1 to a Vec<Handle> of length num_handles; sending consumes the Handle.
  • Detection: Rust ownership makes double-close a compile error in safe code; debug builds tag each Handle with origin + a process-wide live-FD registry asserting no two Handles share a raw fd and counts reconcile; ASan/valgrind in CI; test that a non-passed FD is CLOEXEC (child probe).
  • Telemetry: ipc_handles_sent_total, ipc_handles_recv_total, handle_close_total, handle_leak_suspected_total; span attr num_handles on every IPC span; assert sent==declared.
  • Recovery: mismatch between num_handles and received FDs → reject the whole Message (INV-9), close any received FDs, drop. A detected double-close attempt is a TCB bug → abort the broker in debug, log+continue+alert in release.
  • Debug: dump the live-FD registry (raw fd → owning Handle origin); compare header.num_handles vs cmsg count; strace -e trace=recvmsg the broker; check /proc/<pid>/fd for unexpected entries in a child.

INV-9 — IPC framing is bounds-checked CRIT-TB

  • Statement: the wire decoder validates, before any allocation or indexing: frame_len ≤ MAX_MESSAGE (1 MiB); payload_len + header_size == frame_len; num_handles ≤ MAX_HANDLES (64) AND num_handles == received cmsg FD count; route/kind/req_id read from fixed offsets only. No wire length or count sizes a slice or loop without a prior ceiling check.
  • Why: a malicious child fully controls these fields; an unchecked length → OOB read/heap overflow or unbounded alloc (OOM DoS) in the TCB — the most direct memory-corruption escape vector.
  • Enforced: sbx-ipc decoder is total + allocation-bounded: header from a fixed slice, every bound via checked_*/explicit if, Vec::try_reserve with the validated (capped) size; the framer caps payload/handles at send time too.
  • Detection: cargo-fuzz/AFL target on the decoder (Phase 13) — corpus of malformed frames (giant len, len/handle mismatch, truncated, huge num_handles) asserts never-panic, never-OOB, never-alloc>cap, returns Err; #![forbid(unsafe_code)] in the decode module (or audited unsafe + proptest).
  • Telemetry: ipc_frame_reject_total{reason=oversize|len_mismatch|handle_mismatch|truncated}; span ipc.decode{frame_len, num_handles, verdict}.
  • Recovery: any failed bound → drop the frame, close received FDs, bump reject; a stream of rejects from one peer is hostile/buggy → close the pipe (→ INV-7 disconnect path).
  • Debug: persist the rejected frame (hex, capped) + reason; minimize via the fuzzer; assert the reproduced frame yields the same Err with no UB under ASan.

INV-10 — Zygote child seals sandbox before untrusted data CRIT-TB

  • Statement: a child fork()ed from the ForkServer MUST complete sandbox sealing/tightening (apply the per-type Policy: tighten seccomp, finalize namespaces/rootfs view, re-confirm caps==0, no_new_privs) BEFORE it reads or acts on the first byte of navigation/IPC data from the browser or any peer.
  • Why: the zygote is pre-forked with a looser base policy to share COW pages; if a forked renderer touches untrusted input before tightening, it runs hostile work at the zygote's broader authority — escape.
  • Enforced: sbx-zygote::ForkServer services ForkRequest{process_type, policy, channel}: in the child after fork it calls LinuxSandbox::apply(&policy) (sealing seccomp etc.) and only THEN hands channel to the renderer entrypoint; the renderer's first recv is unreachable until apply returns Ok (else INV-5 abort).
  • Detection: sentinel — renderer-main asserts (debug) seccomp_mode==2 and CapEff==0 on entry; ordering test injects a "read before seal" and asserts it's impossible/aborts; RED-TEAM forks a renderer and immediately probes a syscall the base policy allows but the sealed policy forbids → must be killed.
  • Telemetry: zygote_fork_total{type}, zygote_seal_total{type}, zygote_seal_latency_ms; ordered spans zygote.fork → zygote.seal → renderer.start (seal MUST precede start).
  • Recovery: seal failure in the forked child → abort (INV-5); the browser sees spawn failure, falls back to direct clone/exec spawn.
  • Debug: compare base vs sealed Policy (BPF diff, ns diff); inspect /proc/<pid>/status at the renderer-start sentinel; sbx spawn --use-zygote --dump-seal.

7.3 Isolation & safety invariants (HIGH)

INV-7 — Renderer crash cannot crash the browser or a sibling HIGH

  • Statement: a child SIGSEGV/SIGABRT/SIGSYS/_exit or IPC disconnect is observed by the browser and contained: the browser and all other children keep running; no shared mutable state is corrupted; only the dead child's routes/host are torn down.
  • Why: process isolation is the point of multi-process — one tab's bug/compromise must not be a whole-browser outage or a vector into siblings.
  • Enforced: children are separate processes (no shared address space — only typed IPC + explicit sealed shm); sbx-crash::CrashHandler watches SIGCHLD/waitpid + IPC EOF; the browser never blocks indefinitely on a child (recv timeouts); the broker treats EPIPE/ECONNRESET from a dead child as a normal Err, never unwrap.
  • Detection: sbx crash-test forces a renderer crash (test hook in sbx-renderer) asserting: browser alive, sibling alive, dead child's RenderProcessHost removed, "sad tab" signaled; chaos test crashes children in a loop under load.
  • Telemetry: child_crash_total{type, signal}, child_disconnect_total, sad_tab_total, browser_uptime_seconds (must not reset); spans crash.detect{pid, signal} → crash.cleanup → restart_decision.
  • Recovery: RestartPolicy (Never/Restart{max,window}/ReloadTab) governs respawn; "sad tab" surfaced; routes reaped; shm unmapped. Crash-loop (exceeds max in window) → stop restarting, leave sad tab.
  • Debug: capture Minidump (pid, signal, regs-ish, maps); correlate with the last decoded Message/BrokerRequest from that child; check for a poisoned shared SharedBuffer (should be impossible — seals).

INV-8 — Cross-site isolated docs never co-located HIGH

  • Statement: two documents whose sites differ (site = scheme + eTLD+1 via the public-suffix list) and that are NOT in the same BrowsingInstance with a scripting relationship are NEVER hosted by one renderer process. Assignment is process-per-SiteInstance.
  • Why: co-locating cross-site documents puts another origin's data in a compromised renderer's address space → defeats Site Isolation (Spectre/UXSS-class cross-origin theft).
  • Enforced: sbx-site::SiteIsolationPolicy::site_for(url) derives the site; sbx-browser maps SiteInstance → process and, on navigation, assigns/reuses per ProcessReusePolicy (ProcessPerSiteInstance default, ReuseExistingForSite, LimitReached); a process hosts only SiteInstances from one site (or same-BrowsingInstance scriptable peers).
  • Detection: unit tests on eTLD+1 derivation (multi-label public suffixes, IP hosts, file:/opaque schemes, punycode); invariant test: after N cross-site navigations no process's host set mixes distinct sites; debug_assert! site match at assignment time.
  • Telemetry: site_instances_total, process_per_site_ratio, cross_site_colocation_total (MUST be 0), process_reuse_total{reason}; span nav.assign{url, site, reused?, pid}.
  • Recovery: on the process-count limit (LimitReached), reuse is allowed ONLY within the same site — never across sites (correctness > process savings). A detected colocation is a P1 bug → split.
  • Debug: dump the site → process map + each host's hosted SiteIds; sbx sites lists assignments; re-derive site_for on the offending URL pair.

INV-11 — ProcessHost registry ⇔ live children HIGH

  • Statement: every entry in sbx-browser::ProcessHost corresponds to a live (or being-reaped) child PID, and every spawned child has exactly one registry entry; no dangling host after exit, no orphan child without a host.
  • Why: dangling hosts route messages to dead PIDs (or PID-reuse → wrong process); orphan children are unsupervised, un-sandbox-audited authority leaks.
  • Enforced: spawn (ProcessLauncher::launch) + registry insert are one transaction; CrashHandler removal on SIGCHLD also clears routes; PIDs are never reused for routing within a host's lifetime (validate via the IPC-handshake nonce, not bare PID).
  • Detection: debug audit walks ProcessHost vs /proc + waitpid set asserting a bijection; leak test spawns/kills many children, asserts registry size returns to baseline.
  • Telemetry: process_count{type}, host_orphan_total (0), host_dangling_total (0); spans host.insert/host.remove.
  • Recovery: orphan found → reap + sandbox-audit + record incident; dangling host → remove + drop its routes.
  • Debug: diff registry vs live PIDs; check handshake nonce vs PID for reuse; trace the missing insert/remove span.

INV-13 — Renderer holds no self-open-able path FD HIGH

  • Statement: a renderer's FD set contains only: the IPC channel, broker-granted FDs (SCM_RIGHTS, INV-6), and mapped SharedBuffers — never a directory FD or path it could openat() to widen access; and open/openat are blocked by seccomp/empty rootfs anyway.
  • Why: capability = the FD itself; a stray path/dir FD + an allowed openat would bypass the broker's path-glob check (INV-4).
  • Enforced: sbx-launcher FD sweep (INV-1) + minimal pivot_root + seccomp denying open/openat/open_by_handle_at for renderers; the broker returns only the specific file FD requested.
  • Detection: child probes /proc/self/fd (debug) asserting only expected FDs; RED-TEAM attempts openat(AT_FDCWD,…) and via any inherited dir FD → expects EACCES/SIGSYS.
  • Telemetry: renderer_open_attempt_total (~0 / all denied); span on the seccomp open kill.
  • Recovery: any unexpected FD at start → abort (INV-5); an allowed-but-unexpected dir FD is a launcher bug → fix the sweep.
  • Debug: enumerate /proc/<pid>/fd for the renderer; map each to its Handle origin; confirm none are O_PATH/dir.

INV-14 — Two layers, each independently fail-safe HIGH

  • Statement: the seccomp syscall filter and the broker policy are independent defenses; correctness does NOT depend on both — a bug defeating one layer must still leave the other denying.
  • Why: any single sandbox layer has historically had bugs; the model's resilience is that an escape requires defeating BOTH at once.
  • Enforced: layers share no decision code/state — seccomp is a kernel cBPF program; the broker is a userspace PolicyEngine; resources blocked at the kernel (network in renderer) are also not grantable absent the matching Capability.
  • Detection: "single-layer-off" tests: disable seccomp in a harness → broker still denies un-capable ops; neuter a broker rule in test → seccomp still blocks the raw syscall. Both asserted independently.
  • Telemetry: layer_block_total{layer=seccomp|broker}; correlate that no resource is reachable when either is the only active denier.
  • Recovery: discovering a resource reachable with one layer off ⇒ P0: the other layer is missing a rule — add it; layers must overlap on every sensitive resource.
  • Debug: matrix test (resource × layer-disabled) — every cell must still be denied by the remaining layer; map gaps.

7.4 Correctness / liveness invariants (MED)

INV-12 — req_id uniqueness; no stray responses MED

  • Statement: within one MessagePipe, an outstanding request's req_id is unique; a response is delivered only to a still-open, matching req_id; a response for an unknown/closed req_id is dropped, not dispatched.
  • Why: mismatched correlation → a child could spoof/mis-route a response (confused-deputy-lite) or trigger use-after-free of a completed request slot.
  • Enforced: sbx-ipc allocates monotonic req_id per pipe; a pending-request map gates response dispatch; unknown req_id → drop + metric.
  • Detection: test injects duplicate/unknown req_id asserting no double-completion, no panic; fuzz includes random req_id.
  • Telemetry: ipc_response_orphan_total, ipc_pending_inflight; span attr req_id on request/response pairs.
  • Recovery: drop orphan responses; a flood from a child is hostile → close the pipe (INV-7).
  • Debug: dump the pending-request map vs incoming req_id; trace request→response correlation spans.

7.5 Cross-cutting enforcement & debugging methodology

Mechanism Applies to Notes
Type system / ownership (Rust) INV-3, INV-6, INV-9 move-only Handle, typed decode, #![forbid(unsafe_code)] in decoders
debug_assert! self-checks INV-1, INV-2, INV-5, INV-10, INV-11 stripped in release but gate CI
Runtime checks (always on) INV-4, INV-9, INV-7, INV-12 bounds, deny-by-default, EOF handling — never compiled out
Fuzzing (Phase 13) INV-9, INV-3, INV-12 malformed Message/BrokerRequest ⇒ never crash TCB
RED-TEAM sbx attack (Phase 13) INV-1,2,4,8,10,13,14 forbidden syscall/file/socket/colocation ⇒ MUST be denied/killed; the security "done" gate
Telemetry assertions all "must-be-0" counters (cross_site_colocation_total, host_orphan_total, double-close) alert immediately

General methodology when an invariant is suspected violated: (1) classify severity — any CRIT-TB violation is a P0 security incident: freeze + preserve state; (2) snapshot the offending child's /proc/<pid>/{status,maps,mountinfo,fd} + seccomp mode + Minidump; (3) correlate the trace (sandbox.apply / ipc.decode / broker.policy spans) with the last validated input; (4) reproduce in isolation via the matching sbx subcommand (sandbox-test, ipc-ping --raw, policy --pid, attack, crash-test); (5) confirm the other defense layer (INV-14) still holds; (6) add a regression to the conformance/fuzz/RED-TEAM suite BEFORE fixing — the suite is the durable proof the invariant is restored.

8. Domain Model — Processes, IPC Protocol, Sandbox Policy, and the Site Model

Five coupled sub-models meeting at the trust boundary: above = browser/broker = TCB; below = fully untrusted sandboxed children. Every cross-boundary fact (Message, brokered FD, process assignment) is named here once; phases reuse it.

8.1 Process taxonomy

ProcessType Role Trust Network ns Default CapabilitySet Sandbox tightness
Browser Privileged TCB host net n/a (is the broker) none — it IS the authority
Renderer Sandboxed hostile NET ns (no net) {} empty → all via broker strictest: full ns + tight seccomp
Gpu Sandboxed semi NET ns {GpuChannel, SharedMemory} relaxed: needs ioctl/DRM nodes
Network Sandboxed semi host net {ConnectSocket{*}} relaxed: socket/connect/sendto allowed
Utility Sandboxed minimal NET ns per-task narrow grant strict, task-scoped
Storage Sandboxed minimal NET ns {OpenFile{<profile>/**}} strict + path-glob limited

Principal = SiteInstance (§8.6), not URL/tab. One RenderProcessHost ↔ one child ↔ one SiteInstance (subject to ProcessReusePolicy).

8.2 Process lifecycle state machine

Spawning → Handshaking → Sandboxed → Live → {Crashed | Exited} → Reaped

From Event To TCB action / invariant
— launch(LaunchOptions) Spawning clone/exec or zygote ForkRequest; allocate ProcessId, socketpair → MessagePipe
Spawning child applied sandbox + self-check ok Handshaking child sealed (INV-1/10) BEFORE this edge; sends ready Message
Spawning sandbox-apply fail / no ready within T_handshake(=500ms) Crashed fail closed (INV-5): never run unconfined; SpawnFailed
Handshaking handshake Message validated (proto ver, type echo) Sandboxed confirm sealed=true; bind Endpoint<T> routes
Sandboxed first route bound, broker reachable Live serve traffic; only state that runs untrusted work
Live SIGCHLD w/ signal | IPC EOF/disconnect Crashed sbx-crash: capture Minidump, sad-tab (INV-7)
Live clean exit(0) Exited normal teardown
Crashed/Exited waitpid reaped + routes torn down Reaped free ProcessId (no reuse — anti-ABA), apply RestartPolicy

Sandbox seal is a precondition of Handshaking, never a later edge → no window where a Live/handshaking child is unconfined.

8.3 IPC wire format

Transport = one MessagePipe per child = a socketpair(AF_UNIX, SOCK_SEQPACKET, 0) (SEQPACKET → message boundaries preserved, no stream re-framing ambiguity; datagram cap respected, large payloads go via sbx-shm). Each Message = one sendmsg; handles ride the ancillary control buffer via SCM_RIGHTS.

Wire layout (little-endian, header fixed 24 bytes):

 byte 0          4          8                 16                22   24
 +----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+
 | route (u32)  | kind(u16)| req_id (u64, correlation)         | nh |pad|   <- MessageHeader (24B)
 +----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+
 | payload[payload_len] (CBOR/postcard-encoded args)                   |   <- Message.payload
 +---------------------------------------------------------------------+
   ancillary (separate cmsg, NOT in payload):
   cmsg_level=SOL_SOCKET  cmsg_type=SCM_RIGHTS  data=[fd_0 .. fd_{nh-1}]  <- Message.handles
  • route: RouteId — demux key WITHIN this pipe (§8.7); validated owned-by-sender (INV-14).
  • kind: MsgKind — Request | Response | Event | ControlReady | ControlBye.
  • req_id: u64 — correlation. Request allocates monotonic id; matching Response echoes it. Event ⇒ req_id=0. One id ↔ ≤1 in-flight response (INV-13: dup/unknown id rejected → no request smuggling).
  • num_handles (nh): u16 — count of FDs in the ancillary cmsg. Decode invariant (INV-9): nh MUST equal the FD count actually delivered by the kernel in the cmsg; mismatch → reject frame, close any received FDs, SbxError::Ipc. Hard cap nh ≤ 8; payload_len ≤ 64 KiB (overflow → shm).

Framing/bounds: header parsed first; reject if payload_len > cap or nh > cap or trailing bytes ≠ payload_len. No raw payload pointer is treated as a trusted struct — always deserialized through a schema (INV-3).

Interface/Endpoint<T>: a typed contract over one route. Endpoint<T: Interface> = sugar that (de)serializes T's methods to Messages; Mojo-like. pair() -> (MessagePipe, MessagePipe) = the socketpair primitive.

8.4 Sandbox policy model

Dispositions (Disposition):

Variant seccomp ret Meaning
Allow SECCOMP_RET_ALLOW syscall proceeds
Errno(e) SECCOMP_RET_ERRNO|e fail w/ errno, no kill (graceful, e.g. EPERM)
Trap SECCOMP_RET_TRAP SIGSYS → handler (log/emulate)
KillProcess SECCOMP_RET_KILL_PROCESS whole process dies (the default for forbidden)
Log SECCOMP_RET_LOG allow + audit (dev only)

seccomp_data (filter input, read-only):

struct seccomp_data { int nr; __u32 arch; __u64 instruction_pointer; __u64 args[6]; }

Filter shape (cBPF): insn[0] loads arch; if arch != AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64 → KILL_PROCESS (blocks x32/ia32 ABI bypass, INV-2) BEFORE any nr dispatch. Installed only after PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS=1 (else seccomp()→EINVAL). Default tail = KillProcess ⇒ deny by default.

Namespaces clone flags (renderer): CLONE_NEWUSER | CLONE_NEWPID | CLONE_NEWNET | CLONE_NEWNS | CLONE_NEWIPC | CLONE_NEWUTS. USER first (unprivileged → fake root → enables the rest); NET = no network (forces brokering); NS+pivot_root = minimal read-mostly rootfs.

Baseline renderer SyscallPolicy (pseudo-DSL excerpt):

arch  != AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64           => KillProcess     # INV-2 guard, insn 0
read, write, recvmsg, sendmsg        => Allow           # talk to broker/parent only
mmap(prot) if !(prot & PROT_EXEC)    => Allow           # W^X: executable maps denied
mprotect(prot) if !(prot & PROT_EXEC)=> Allow
futex, nanosleep, sched_yield, brk   => Allow
exit, exit_group, rt_sigreturn       => Allow
close, fstat, lseek, ppoll, epoll_*  => Allow
clone(flags) if flags & CLONE_THREAD => Allow           # threads ok
clone(flags) otherwise               => Errno(EPERM)    # no new process
open, openat, creat                  => KillProcess     # no direct FS — broker only
socket, connect, bind, sendto        => KillProcess     # no direct net — NET ns + kill
execve, execveat, ptrace, mount      => KillProcess
fork, vfork, clone(NEWUSER)          => KillProcess     # no sandbox unshare
*  (unlisted)                        => KillProcess     # deny by default

Two independent layers (defense in depth): (1) seccomp syscall filter, (2) broker CapabilitySet check. A bug in either alone must not yield escape. Forbidden actions are killed at the syscall layer; legitimate resource needs are routed to the broker (FD-passing), never to a direct syscall.

8.5 Capability model

Capability variants (least-privilege grants):

Variant Authorizes Validated against op
OpenFile{path_glob, mode: AccessMode} open files matching glob, ≤ mode canonicalized path re-matched vs glob (INV-4)
ConnectSocket{host, port} TCP connect to host:port exact host+port match
SharedMemory{max_bytes} map a SharedBuffer ≤ max_bytes requested len ≤ max_bytes
ReadClipboard read clipboard contents presence check
GpuChannel obtain the GPU IPC channel presence check

CapabilitySet = the per-process set; assigned at launch from the ProcessType profile + navigation context. Immutable for the child's lifetime (INV-11): no in-process grant/revoke; widening authority = a new process.

Grant/validate flow:

launch: BrowserProcess derives CapabilitySet(process_type, site) -> PolicyEngine.bind(pid -> caps)
runtime: child -> BrokerRequest{from=pid, op=OpenFile{"/p"}}  --IPC-->  Broker.service
         PolicyEngine.validate(op, caps): canonicalize path, find matching Capability
           match  -> open()/connect()/memfd -> BrokerResponse::Handle(fd via SCM_RIGHTS)  # FD-only, INV-12
           none   -> BrokerResponse::Denied("no cap for /p")                              # deny by default

Returned FDs never carry a path/name back (INV-12): the renderer gets bearer authority (a Handle), not a namespace it can enumerate.

8.6 Site model

site = scheme + eTLD+1 (registrable domain), eTLD computed via the public-suffix list (PSL). SiteIsolationPolicy::site_for(url) -> SiteId.

URL scheme host eTLD (PSL) eTLD+1 SiteId
https://a.example.com/x https a.example.com com example.com https://example.com
https://b.example.com/y https b.example.com com example.com https://example.com ← same site
https://foo.github.io/ https foo.github.io github.io (PSL!) foo.github.io https://foo.github.io ← isolated from bar.github.io
http://example.com http example.com com example.com http://example.com ← scheme differs → distinct

SiteInstance = a SiteId within a BrowsingInstance. BrowsingInstance = a set of SiteInstances that may script each other (window.opener-reachable) ⇒ same-instance same-site documents may share a process. Cross-BrowsingInstance never shares (INV-8 candidates isolated).

Process assignment (ProcessReusePolicy): ProcessPerSiteInstance (default) → one renderer per SiteInstance. ReuseExistingForSite → if a live host already serves this SiteId in the same BrowsingInstance, reuse it. LimitReached → at the process-count cap, coalesce same-site tabs onto one host.

Reuse example: tab1→https://example.com, tab2 (same BrowsingInstance)→https://b.example.com. Both map to SiteId https://example.com ⇒ same RenderProcessHost reused (one process, two RouteIds). tab3→https://foo.github.io ⇒ distinct SiteId ⇒ new sandboxed renderer.

8.7 RouteId multiplexing

One MessagePipe per child carries many logical frames; RouteId demuxes (one per RenderFrame). RouteId is namespaced per pipe — same numeric value on two pipes is unrelated, and never crosses the process boundary (INV-14: a route is owned by exactly one host). Browser router dispatches inbound Message by (pid, route).

8.8 End-to-end worked example — navigate https://a.example

1. NAV     BrowserProcess receives navigate(tab=7, url="https://a.example")
2. SITE    SiteIsolationPolicy.site_for -> SiteId("https://a.example")   # eTLD+1 = a.example via PSL
3. ASSIGN  ProcessReusePolicy=ProcessPerSiteInstance, no live host for site
           -> new SiteInstance{ id, site, browsing_instance } ; new RenderProcessHost{ pid?, site_instance, routes:[r0] }
4. SPAWN   ProcessLauncher.launch(LaunchOptions{ Renderer, policy=renderer_profile, use_zygote=true })
           -> ForkRequest to Zygote (COW fork, fast) ; child SEALS sandbox (ns+pivot_root+caps+no_new_privs+seccomp)
           -> child self-check ok -> Handshaking -> ready Message -> Sandboxed -> Live   (states §8.2)
5. BIND    browser binds Endpoint<FrameHost> on route r0 ; sends Navigate{route:r0, url}
6. BROKER  renderer needs /usr/share/fonts/x.ttf -> BrokerRequest{from=pid, op=OpenFile{"/usr/share/fonts/*.ttf", Read}}
           PolicyEngine: renderer CapabilitySet has OpenFile{"/usr/share/fonts/**",Read} -> canonicalize -> match
           -> open(O_RDONLY) -> BrokerResponse::Handle(fd) via SCM_RIGHTS   # FD-only
7. ATTACK  compromised renderer calls open("/etc/shadow") DIRECTLY (not broker)
           -> seccomp baseline: open => KillProcess -> SIGKILL ; SIGCHLD -> Crashed -> Minidump -> sad tab
           browser + every other renderer UNAFFECTED (blast radius = this child's CapabilitySet only)

8.9 Malicious / edge cases — required outcomes

Stimulus Layer Outcome
renderer issues open()/socket() directly seccomp SECCOMP_RET_KILL_PROCESS → SIGKILL → sad tab
BrokerRequest for path/host outside CapabilitySet broker PolicyEngine BrokerResponse::Denied(reason), logged; surge → mark host suspect → terminate (INV-4)
Message with num_handles=3 but cmsg carries 1 FD sbx-ipc decode reject frame, close stray FDs, SbxError::Ipc; no OOB (INV-9)
payload_len > 64 KiB or trailing-byte mismatch sbx-ipc decode reject; never allocate attacker-chosen huge buffer
duplicate/unknown req_id on a Response sbx-ipc correlation drop, no smuggling (INV-13)
inbound route not owned by sending host sbx-browser router reject, SbxError::Ipc (INV-14)
clone(CLONE_NEWUSER) from renderer (re-sandbox/escape try) seccomp KillProcess
x32-ABI syscall to dodge the filter seccomp insn 0 arch mismatch → KillProcess (INV-2)
renderer crashes (segfault / forced via test hook) sbx-crash SIGCHLD/IPC-EOF → Crashed → Minidump + sad tab; RestartPolicy applied; browser + peers isolated (INV-7)
renderer tries to grow a sealed SharedBuffer sbx-shm seals ftruncate→EPERM (F_SEAL_GROW/SHRINK set before share, INV-15)

Every row resolves to deny / kill / reject / isolate — never to silent escalation. The two layers (§8.4) ensure a single-layer bug still leaves the other intact.

9. Tech Stack ADR Table

Architecture decisions for project 266. All LOCKED. Status: Accepted unless marked PhaseDeferred (deferred work behind a stable trait, not a reversal). Trust-boundary-relevant decisions are flagged [TCB].

ADR # Decision Locked Choice Rejected Alternatives Rationale When the Rejected Choice Wins
01 Implementation language [TCB] Rust, edition 2021, MSRV 1.78 C/C++ (Chromium's language); Go; any GC scripting lang Broker is the TCB parsing fully-untrusted renderer input → memory safety eliminates Chromium's dominant sandbox-CVE class (UAF/OOB in the broker). Need raw fork/clone/seccomp/namespace control with safe handle wrappers. C/C++ reintroduces the exact attack surface we remove. Go's runtime + M:N scheduler fights single-threaded clone/seccomp/unshare ordering; GC + thread-per-filter friction. C/C++ wins when the codebase is already a multi-million-line C++ engine (Chromium itself) and the incremental security gain of Rust is outweighed by interop friction; C wins in resource-constrained embedded systems where std Rust is unavailable and the sandbox perimeter is hardware-enforced anyway.
02 Code organization Cargo workspace, 12 members sbx-* one monolith crate; one crate per binary Crate boundaries enforce the layering invariant: sbx-core is OS-free; sbx-ipc/sbx-shm/sbx-sandbox/sbx-broker are independently testable + fuzzable; the dependency DAG is the architecture. Monolith blurs the trust boundary in the type system. A single crate wins for a small prototype or a component that will always be used as a whole — when crate-boundary compile overhead and version management cost outweigh the layering benefit. One-crate-per-binary wins when each binary is genuinely unrelated and sharing types has negative value.
03 OS target priority [TCB] Linux primary; Sandbox trait abstracts backend cross-platform from day 1; Linux-only with no trait Confinement primitives (seccomp-bpf, namespaces, pivot_root) are Linux-specific; chasing 3 OSes dilutes depth. The Sandbox trait + LinuxSandbox lets macOS (Seatbelt) / Windows (job object + restricted token + AppContainer) ship as PhaseDeferred stubs without API churn. Cross-platform from day 1 wins when the deployment target is an existing multi-OS product (e.g., a shipped browser or IDE plugin) where macOS/Windows usage volume exceeds Linux; the Sandbox trait abstraction is then the right design but must be fleshed out in all backends immediately.
04 macOS / Windows backends PhaseDeferred stubs behind Sandbox full parity now; #[cfg] forks everywhere Keeps one confinement contract; defers OS-specific work without leaking platform cfg into callers. Stubs return SbxError::Sandbox (fail-closed), never silently un-sandboxed. Full parity now wins when the primary users are on macOS or Windows (consumer browser distribution) and shipping a non-functional stub creates a worse user experience than shipping a weaker but working sandbox. #[cfg] forks win in a tiny two-platform tool where the abstraction overhead exceeds the sharing benefit.
05 IPC transport [TCB] UNIX domain socket (socketpair) per pipe + SCM_RIGHTS anonymous pipes; shared-memory-only; D-Bus / a message bus UDS gives bidirectional framed datagrams and ancillary FD passing in one primitive — the capability-transfer mechanism the broker needs. Pipes can't pass FDs. Shm-only lacks a control channel + handshake. A bus adds a third trusted daemon inside the trust boundary. Anonymous pipes win when FD transfer is never needed and message sizes are small and one-directional (logging, streaming stdout). A message bus (D-Bus, systemd socket activation) wins in a desktop-integration context where services must be discoverable, activated on demand, and share a well-known namespace. Shared-memory-only wins when throughput dominates and a separate control channel is already established.
06 Handle passing [TCB] SCM_RIGHTS FD duplication, wrapped in Handle path strings in messages; pidfd_getfd; /proc/pid/fd Passing an FD is passing a capability (broker opens a file, sends only the FD — never a path the renderer could re-derive or TOCTOU). Handle owns the fd, closes on drop, moves on send. Path-in-message would reopen the confused-deputy hole brokering exists to close. Path strings win when the peer process is trusted (same privilege level, no adversarial input) and the path is stable enough that re-opening it on the peer side is safe — for example, inter-trusted-service IPC in a same-UID microservice mesh where TOCTOU risk is not in scope. pidfd_getfd wins when transferring fds into a process you did not fork and cannot send a socket message to (post-hoc fd injection during debugging or tooling).
07 IPC typing layer Mojo-like Interface + Endpoint<T> over Message{header,payload,handles} raw bytes ad hoc; gRPC; protobuf-over-socket; Cap'n Proto Typed request/response + async with a hand-rolled compact wire format = no codegen toolchain, no large transitive dep tree in the TCB, full control of the parser we fuzz. gRPC/protobuf pull HTTP/2 + heavy deps into the broker. Models Mojo/IPDL directly. gRPC wins in a multi-language, multi-service microservice environment where schema evolution, cross-language stubs, and load balancing are priorities — the toolchain cost is amortized. Cap'n Proto wins for zero-copy RPC where message parsing latency dominates. Raw bytes win in ultra-constrained embedded systems where any framework is overhead.
08 Wire serialization Hand-written framing over seccomp_data-style fixed header + length-prefixed payload serde + bincode; protobuf; JSON The deserializer sits on the trust boundary; a small, audited, fuzzed parser beats a large generic one. Fixed MessageHeader{route,kind,req_id,num_handles}; num_handles validated against received SCM_RIGHTS count (mismatch → drop + close). serde + bincode wins for trusted inter-service IPC in the same trust domain where developer velocity outweighs minimal TCB size and the serializer does not parse adversarial input. protobuf/JSON win when the protocol must be stable across major version changes, readable by external tools, or consumed by multiple language ecosystems.
09 Shared memory [TCB] memfd_create + F_SEAL_* (SEAL_SHRINK/GROW/WRITE), SharedBuffer/Mapping/ShmRing POSIX shm_open; mmap of a tmpfs file; SysV shm memfd has no filesystem name → nothing for a sandboxed renderer to reopen; passed as an FD via SCM_RIGHTS. Seals make a producer-frozen buffer immutable so the broker can map RO without a renderer racing the size/content (defeats shm TOCTOU). POSIX/SysV shm need a namespace path + leak across the boundary. POSIX shm_open wins when the communicating processes are unrelated (no common ancestor to forward an FD) and must discover the shared segment by name in a controlled namespace. SysV shm wins in legacy codebases or when communicating with non-Linux systems that do not support memfd.
10 Large transfer path ShmRing SPSC ring over a sealed SharedBuffer copy every payload through the socket; one memfd per message Bulk/zero-copy frames bypass the socket copy; control + small msgs stay on the typed pipe. SPSC needs no lock; head/tail are the only shared mutable words, bounded + validated each read. Per-message copy through the socket wins when message sizes are small (< 4 KB) and frequency is low — the socket copy is a single sendmsg, cheaper than allocating + sealing a memfd. One memfd per message wins for irregular, bursty large transfers where a persistent ring adds coordination overhead and wasted ring space.
11 seccomp-bpf builder [TCB] Hand-built cBPF builder in SeccompFilter (compiles SyscallRule[]) libseccomp (C lib); seccompiler crate Owning the cBPF emitter removes a C dependency from the TCB, guarantees the arch-check-first instruction ordering, and lets the red-team harness assert the exact emitted program. libseccomp is C (memory-unsafety) + abstracts away ordering we must control; seccompiler is reasonable but we want a fully-audited, dependency-light filter we can prove. libseccomp wins when the policy is complex, multi-arch, and the team has limited BPF expertise — the C library's abstraction + portability outweighs the dependency cost for a non-Rust-TCB system. seccompiler wins when a Rust-native cBPF compiler is preferred but the arch-check + ordering requirements are met by the crate's API, avoiding the maintenance burden of a hand-rolled emitter.
12 seccomp default disposition [TCB] Deny by default: unlisted syscall → KillProcess; explicit Allow/Errno/Trap/Log per rule allowlist-by-exception over a permissive base; Errno default Allowlist closes the whole syscall ABI; an unknown/new syscall is denied, not silently permitted. KillProcess (not Trap) for the renderer baseline so a forbidden call is fatal + observable, not catchable. Errno default wins for development environments where killing the process on every new syscall blocks iteration — Errno surfaces breakage in test logs without crashing; Trap (SIGSYS) wins when the sandbox must intercept forbidden calls for telemetry or forwarding to the broker without terminating the child (user-notify design). Permissive base wins only in controlled environments where a first-pass audit is preferred before tightening.
13 seccomp arch handling [TCB] Check seccomp_data.arch first; allow only the expected AUDIT_ARCH_*; deny x32/ia32 trust the syscall nr alone Syscall numbers differ per ABI; without an arch gate, an attacker re-enters via the x32 or i386 compat ABI to invoke a number that maps to an allowed name on the native ABI. First BPF instruction rejects any non-native arch. Trusting the syscall number alone is acceptable only on a host kernel configured with no compat ABI support (CONFIG_X86_X32_ABI=n, CONFIG_IA32_EMULATION=n) — where only one ABI is possible, the arch check is redundant (but still best practice to future-proof).
14 seccomp install ordering [TCB] PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS before installing the filter rely on privilege; install filter first no_new_privs lets an unprivileged process load a filter and blocks setuid/fcaps from regaining privilege through the filter; required precondition for unprivileged seccomp. Installing without no_new_privs wins only when the process holds CAP_SYS_ADMIN (e.g., a privileged container-setup tool) and the use of no_new_privs would block legitimate privilege transitions elsewhere in the lifecycle — a constrained operational scenario where the process needs to gain privilege after filter install.
15 Privilege model for namespaces [TCB] Unprivileged user namespace maps child→fake root, enabling pid/net/mnt/ipc/uts ns SUID/setuid helper binary; CAP-granted launcher; no namespaces No SUID bit, no real root, no fcaps to audit — the user ns grants the appearance of root inside the child only, which is enough to unshare the rest. Removes an entire privileged-helper attack surface present in older Chromium SUID-sandbox designs. SUID helper wins on enterprise Linux kernels where /proc/sys/kernel/unprivileged_userns_clone is locked to 0 by policy (common in RHEL/CentOS hardening profiles) — user namespaces are disabled system-wide, making a setuid helper the only available path. CAP-granted launcher wins in container runtimes that already run as root or with elevated caps.
16 Filesystem confinement [TCB] pivot_root to a minimal, mostly-RO rootfs in a mount ns chroot; bind-mount allowlist only pivot_root detaches the old root so it cannot be re-reached; chroot is escapable (chroot-then-../fd tricks, no detach of old root). Renderer sees a minimal tree; everything real comes via the broker. chroot wins in environments where mount namespaces are unavailable (old kernels, restricted container configs) and the threat model does not include a local-root attacker who can chroot-escape; it remains usable as a path-namespace limiter in those contexts. Bind-mount allowlist wins in container environments where the full OS filesystem is already trusted and only selective path isolation is needed.
17 Renderer spawn path sbx-zygote fork server (warm COW template) fresh clone+exec per renderer; thread pool Fork from a pre-warmed, pre-sandboxed zygote shares read-only pages COW → spawn is a cheap fork, not a cold exec + relink. Each forked child seals/tightens its seccomp policy before touching untrusted work. Target spawn latency dominated by fork, not image load. Fresh clone+exec wins when spawn frequency is low (< 1/s), memory overhead per renderer is acceptable, or the process image changes frequently (e.g., A/B binary experiments) making the COW template stale. Thread pool wins when the workload is trusted (no sandbox needed) and concurrency overhead dominates spawn cost.
18 Site granularity [TCB] Process-per-site-instance (site = scheme + eTLD+1) process-per-tab; process-per-origin; single process The web security principal is the site (registrable domain), and same-BrowsingInstance SiteInstances can script each other so must share a process; per-origin over-fragments + breaks scripting; per-tab ignores cross-tab same-site scripting. Subject to a process-count limit + ProcessReusePolicy. Process-per-tab wins on memory-constrained devices (mobile browsers) where per-site isolation is too expensive and the primary threat is renderer crashes rather than cross-site data exfiltration. Single process wins for a headless/server-side rendering tool with no cross-origin content and where parallelism, not isolation, is the objective.
19 Site derivation source Bundled public suffix list (PSL) → eTLD+1 hardcoded TLD table; live DNS/registry lookup; naive last-2-labels eTLD+1 (a.co.uk ≠ b.co.uk) requires the PSL; naive label-counting mis-groups multi-label suffixes → cross-site sharing = isolation bug. Bundled snapshot = deterministic, offline, audit-pinned; no network in the policy path. Live DNS/registry lookup wins in a trusted enterprise intranet browser where the TLD namespace is internal and controlled — a live lookup against the internal DNS is more accurate than a stale PSL snapshot for internal domains. Hardcoded table wins in an embedded browser with no update mechanism and a known-fixed TLD set.
20 Resource access model [TCB] Capability-based brokering: PolicyEngine validates each BrokerRequest vs the caller's CapabilitySet static path allowlist in the renderer; per-syscall ACL only Capabilities are least-privilege, per-ProcessId, principal = SiteInstance; the broker is the single validation point and returns only a brokered FD. A renderer-side allowlist is inside the untrusted process = worthless. Two independent layers (seccomp and broker) = defense in depth. Static path allowlist wins in a trusted-peer setup where both sides are in the same trust domain and the broker's validation overhead exceeds the benefit (e.g., a local developer tool sandbox where the content is first-party and the threat model excludes malicious content). Per-syscall ACL only wins where there is no broker process and the confinement model is seccomp-only (as in a container runtime that does not implement FD brokering).
21 Fail mode of the sandbox [TCB] Fail-closed: any setup error (ns/seccomp/pivot_root/cap-drop) aborts the child before untrusted code runs best-effort / continue degraded A partially-applied sandbox is worse than none — it looks confined but isn't. Policy::apply returns Err → launcher never hands the child a renderer task; child exits. No "untrusted code with a half-built filter" state exists. Best-effort / degraded wins in a development environment or a tool whose primary value is running the task (not security), where an occasional sandbox setup failure on unusual host configurations should not block the work. Applicable when the sandbox is defense-in-depth on top of other isolation layers (e.g., a VM).
22 Capability dropping [TCB] Drop all capabilities (bounding set + ambient) + no_new_privs in every child drop a curated subset; keep CAP_NET for net process Children need zero kernel capabilities (all privileged ops are brokered); dropping everything removes the privilege-escalation target entirely. The network process gets network access via the broker, not CAP_NET_*. Dropping a curated subset wins when one specific child type has a legitimate need for a narrow capability that the broker cannot proxy — e.g., a GPU process in a system that does not have a DRM/GPU brokering path and requires CAP_SYS_RAWIO for device init. The subset is still least-privilege; the question is whether "zero caps" or "minimal caps" is achievable given the system's broker completeness.
23 IPC multiplexing RouteId-keyed router in sbx-browser over one pipe per child one socket per frame; port-style demux daemon Many RenderFrames multiplex over a single message pipe per process via RouteId in the header — bounds FD count + connection state in the broker, matches Mojo routing. One socket per frame wins when frames are long-lived, large, and independently closeable — a streaming media pipeline where each stream has its own flow control. A port-style demux daemon wins in a microkernel architecture where routing is itself a security domain boundary (capability-based routing through a trusted server).
24 Crash artifact Minidump (pid, signal, register-ish snapshot, maps) via SIGCHLD/exit/IPC-disconnect watch core dumps to disk; full ptrace dumper; no capture A minimal in-process artifact is enough to triage; full core dumps of a sandboxed child leak memory + are heavy. Renderer death is detected ≥1 of three ways; browser stays up, shows sad tab, applies RestartPolicy. Blast radius = that renderer's CapabilitySet. Full core dumps win in an internal development or security research environment where the process is trusted, memory contents are not sensitive, and a full dump enables postmortem debugging in a debugger. Full ptrace dumper wins when registers + stack traces from all threads are needed for production crash analytics (Breakpad/Crashpad pattern). No capture wins in an ultra-constrained embedded system where disk space precludes artifacts.
25 Low-level syscall surface nix + libc (thin, audited usage) only libc (unsafe everywhere); caps/syscallz/nsroots frameworks nix gives safe wrappers over clone/socketpair/sendmsg/mmap/prctl; libc for raw constants + syscalls nix doesn't cover. Avoid heavyweight sandbox frameworks that hide the ordering this design must control. libc-only wins for one-off scripts or tools that need a single syscall and where adding nix as a dependency is disproportionate. Heavyweight frameworks (nsroots, etc.) win for teams without deep Linux kernel expertise who need a higher-level API that handles edge cases, even at the cost of control over ordering — acceptable when the TCB is small and the framework is well-audited.
26 Security testing cargo-fuzz on the IPC Message deserializer + the red-team attack subcommand manual testing only; no fuzzing Fuzzing proves a malformed Message from an untrusted renderer never crashes/UB-s the broker (TCB liveness). The red-team harness (sbx attack) asserts forbidden syscalls/files/sockets are denied or kill the child — the security "done" gate (Phase 13). Manual testing only wins for rapid prototyping where the security boundary is not yet defined and the investment in fuzz infrastructure is premature; acceptable as a temporary state before the TCB surfaces are stabilized. Applicable in a completely closed-source, physically-isolated system where the threat model excludes external attackers.
27 CI GitHub Actions; a privileged Linux runner for namespace/seccomp tests unprivileged runner only; no CI for sandbox Sandbox + namespace tests need an unrestricted kernel surface (user-ns enabled, no host seccomp masking ours); a privileged self-hosted/--privileged runner gates these. Unit/fuzz/build run on standard runners. Unprivileged runner only wins for projects where the sandbox test suite can be fully mocked or skipped — accepting that namespace/seccomp integration tests will only run locally. Acceptable for open-source contributors who lack privileged CI budget. No CI for sandbox tests is a recognized tech-debt posture for early-stage projects where CI infrastructure setup is deferred.
28 Dependency policy [TCB] Minimal, audited tree; cargo-deny + cargo-audit in CI; no unvetted transitive deps in the TCB pull crates freely Every dep in the broker is attack surface; license + advisory + duplicate checks gate merges. Justifies the hand-rolled IPC/seccomp/serialization choices above. Pulling crates freely wins in a non-security-critical application binary or in a rapid-prototype phase where developer velocity is the priority and the TCB boundary has not been finalized — e.g., a UI layer or a test harness binary where a CVE in a dep is low-impact.
29 Licensing MIT OR Apache-2.0 GPL; proprietary Rust-ecosystem standard dual license; permissive, patent-grant via Apache-2.0. GPL wins when the project is intended as a copyleft component that must remain open — when protecting downstream openness is more important than broad commercial adoption. Proprietary wins for a commercial product where the sandbox is a competitive differentiator and the organization can accept the maintenance burden of a closed-source release.

10. Repository Layout

Single Cargo workspace, 12 sbx-* members under crates/. Directory taxonomy mirrors the phase list (Phase 0 → ## 12 … Phase 13 → ## 25): each crate's modules map 1:1 to the LOCKED Core Types; conformance/, fuzz/, benches/ back the ## 25 security/perf gate. Linux backend = real code; macOS/Windows = PhaseDeferred stubs (macos_stub.rs, windows_stub.rs). Spec only — file purposes, no source.

  • browser-sandbox/
    • README.mdpitch · TCB/trust-boundary diagram · quickstart · NON-GOALS (no web engine/net stack/container mgr/hypervisor)

    • LICENSE-APACHEdual license (Apache-2.0)

10.4 conformance/ — golden security fixtures (§17/§25 gate)

  • conformance/
    • seccomp/
      • renderer_baseline.policybaseline renderer SyscallPolicy (allow/errno/kill table) — the reference
      • network.policynetwork-process relaxed policy
      • gpu.policyGPU-process relaxed policy
      • expected/per-policy: syscall -> expected Disposition (assert exact match)
    • attack/
      • forbidden_syscalls.tomlsyscalls a renderer MUST NOT make (ptrace,mount,unshare-newuser,kexec,..) -> expect KILL/ERRNO
      • forbidden_files.tomlpaths a renderer MUST NOT open directly (/etc/shadow,/proc/*/mem,..) -> expect deny
      • forbidden_sockets.tomldirect connect attempts (must be brokered) -> expect ENOSYS/deny
      • arch_bypass.tomlx32/ia32 ABI bypass attempts -> filter MUST reject (arch check first)
    • README.mdhow fixtures run under sandbox-tests.yml; updating a policy requires review (CONTRIBUTING)

10.5 fuzz/ — cargo-fuzz (§25)

  • fuzz/
    • Cargo.tomllibfuzzer-sys harness crate (nightly)
    • fuzz_targets/
      • ipc_message.rsarbitrary bytes -> Message parse · MUST NOT panic/UB; reject cleanly
      • framing.rswire framing decode · length-prefix/bounds · malformed -> error not crash
      • broker_request.rsarbitrary BrokerRequest -> PolicyEngine::service · never escalates, never panics
      • seccomp_builder.rsarbitrary SyscallRule[] -> cBPF compilation · no panic/overflow
      • psl.rsarbitrary host -> eTLD+1 derivation · no panic on weird/Unicode/edge inputs

10.6 Makefile targets (BUILD_PLAN per-phase gate)

make build          # cargo build --workspace (all 12 crates)
make test           # unit + integration (non-privileged subset)
make test-priv      # privileged: real userns/seccomp/pivot_root (needs kernel features; see Dockerfile)
make lint           # cargo fmt --check && cargo clippy --all-targets -D warnings
make deny           # cargo-deny check (licenses + advisories + bans)
make conformance    # run conformance/ seccomp+attack fixtures vs expected/
make attack         # sbx attack red-team harness — MUST exit 0 (all forbidden ops denied/killed)
make fuzz           # short cargo-fuzz run over all fuzz_targets
make bench          # criterion benches vs targets (spawn latency / IPC throughput)
make docker         # build reproducible Linux image
make gate           # build+lint+deny+test+conformance+attack — the per-phase "done" gate

10.8 Layout invariants

Invariant Rule Enforced by
Dep direction sbx-core depends on nothing; sbx-core/sbx-site are OS-free (no nix/libc) Cargo.toml review · deny.toml
TCB boundary only sbx-browser/-broker/-launcher/-zygote/-sandbox run privileged; sbx-renderer never code + sandbox-tests.yml
No code in blueprint this file is spec only; real source lives under crates/ CRITICAL RULE 10
Policy changes reviewed edits to conformance/seccomp/* + templates/policy/* require explicit review CONTRIBUTING + CODEOWNERS
Module ↔ type 1:1 every Core Type has a named home file (above); no orphan types code review
Phase ↔ tree each crate/module introduced in its phase (Phase 0→13 / ## 12→## 25) BUILD_PLAN.md

11. Core Schema and Core Types

Normative type contract. Every phase (## 12..## 25) builds against these signatures. Bodies shown ≤2 illustrative lines; full impl is forbidden (CRITICAL RULE 10). Grouped by crate; dependency order. Universal invariants below apply to all types.

11.0 Universal invariants

  • One process = one address space. No type crosses the trust boundary by reference; cross-process state moves only as a serialized Message or a passed Handle. No shared pointers across the TRUST BOUNDARY.
  • Authority is FD-shaped, not path-shaped. A Capability names a grant; a Handle is the bearer of authority. Holding a Handle to an opened file == authority to use that file. The broker opens, validates, then passes the FD — paths never cross post-grant.
  • Send/Sync discipline: Handle, SharedBuffer, Mapping, Endpoint<T>, MessagePipe are Send, !Sync (own a raw fd / per-thread pipe end). Shared across browser-event-loop tasks only via Mutex/RefCell+single-thread. ProcessId/RouteId/SiteId/Capability/CapabilitySet are Copy/Clone + Send+Sync (plain data).
  • Deny by default: every enum that gates authority (Disposition, BrokerResponse, ProcessReusePolicy) has a denying/erroring variant reachable when no rule matches.
