  • Bench: a tight loop of an allowed syscall (e.g. getpid allowed) with filter vs without → delta = per-syscall BPF cost. Re-run with the policy reordered to prove frequency-ordering helps.
Worst case to avoid: a long unordered filter where the hottest syscall (write) is matched last
→ every write scans the whole program. Pathological: ~Nrules µs/syscall. Fix = reorder.

32.8 Profiling Methodology

Tool Target What it answers
perf record -g + flamegraph IPC hot path, spawn path where self-time goes; is ser/de or syscall dominant
perf stat spawn, sandbox apply cycles, context-switches, page-faults per op
strace -f -c renderer under workload the actual syscall set + frequency → drives seccomp ordering AND the allow-list (a syscall not seen → candidate to deny)
/proc/<pid>/smaps every renderer COW sharing: Pss/Shared_Clean/Private_Dirty
/proc/<pid>/status renderers VmRSS, voluntary_ctxt_switches
ftrace/bpftrace fork path fork→exec→first-instruction latency, scheduler queue delay
  • strace serves two masters: perf (frequency → filter ordering) and security (the observed set is the minimal allow-list; anything outside it is deny-by-default and red-team-tested in Phase 13). Run strace across the full renderer workload to avoid under-counting rarely-hit syscalls.
  • Flamegraph the spawn path to catch surprises (e.g. ld.so symbol resolution in cold exec, uid-map write in user-ns setup).

32.9 Context-Switch & Scheduling Cost of Many Processes

Process-per-site-instance means many processes — the model's cost, paid in scheduler/TLB/cache pressure, not in our code.

  • Each renderer = its own address space → TLB + cache cost on every context switch to/from it; IPC round-trips add 2 voluntary context switches (sender blocks, receiver wakes). This is the structural tax we accept for isolation — quantify it, don't pretend it's zero.
  • Mitigations measured: process count limit + ProcessReusePolicy (don't spawn unboundedly — fewer processes = fewer switches); renderer process priority/nice so a background renderer doesn't steal cycles from the browser; CPU rlimit budgets (Phase 12) cap a runaway renderer's scheduler share.
  • Metric: voluntary_ctxt_switches per renderer per second under a synthetic IPC workload; alert if a renderer's switch rate implies a busy-wait (should block on recvmsg, not spin).

32.10 Tail Latency on Spawn Under Load

The single most important spawn number is p99 under concurrency, because navigations burst (open 20 tabs).

  • sbx bench spawn --concurrency=32 → HdrHistogram. Target p99 ≤40 ms (zygote path).
  • ForkServer is a serialization point: a single fork-server services ForkRequests sequentially. Under burst, requests queue → tail latency grows. Measure queue wait separately from fork cost. Mitigation if p99 misses: bounded request pipelining in the fork server, or a small pool of pre-forked-but-unsealed children claimed on demand (trade idle RSS for tail latency — quantify both sides).
  • Page-cache cold start: first spawn after boot pays disk faults for the rootfs/binary. Warm path (Zygote already faulted-in) is the steady state; report cold-start p99 separately and don't let it pollute the warm SLO.
  • Pitfall: fork() cost grows with the Zygote's page-table size (more mapped pages → more PTEs to COW-mark). Keep the Zygote lean; don't warm pages a renderer won't touch — they cost fork time AND idle RSS for nothing.

32.11 Regression Gating

  • criterion benches run in CI; a statistically-significant regression (> 10% on any §4 micro-target) fails the build. Cross-process custom benches run nightly (too noisy/slow for per-PR) with a wider 20% threshold + trend tracking.
  • Every §4 target has a named bench; "no bench → no claim." The Phase 13 "done" gate requires the spawn/IPC/throughput numbers to be re-confirmed against this table on the reference machine.
  • Numbers feed back: if zygote spawn misses, profile the fork path (§32.8) before relaxing the SLO; if RSS-per-renderer misses, the smaps Private_Dirty breakdown (§32.3) localizes the regression to a dirtied warm page.

Scalability Analysis

Scaling axes: (a) distinct sites/tabs -> process count; (b) IPC fan-out -> central router + browser event loop; (c) spawn rate -> zygote COW; (d) memory pressure -> consolidation. The dominant tension: isolation vs resource — each additional process buys an independent CapabilitySet-bounded blast radius but costs fixed RSS + an fd/process-table slot. There is no free lunch; scaling = bounding process count without collapsing site isolation.

Process count vs distinct sites

Policy = process-per-site-instance (Phase 9), keyed on SiteId = scheme + eTLD+1. Process count grows with distinct sites, not tab count: N tabs of one site = 1 renderer; N tabs of N sites = N renderers. SiteInstances in one BrowsingInstance (script-reachable) co-reside -> sublinear in tabs.

Workload Tabs Distinct sites Renderer processes (uncapped)
Single-site, many tabs 100 1 1
Typical browsing 30 ~20 ~20
Pathological (per-tab unique site) 200 200 min(200, cap)

Bound = hard process cap (default max_renderers = 2·logical_cores, clamp [16, 256]). At cap, ProcessReusePolicy::LimitReached -> ReuseExistingForSite collapses new same-or-arbitrary sites onto existing hosts (security cost: noted below).

Memory is the ceiling

Per-renderer fixed overhead (this minimal stand-in, not a real engine): code/stack/heap + mappings ≈ 2–4 MB RSS private after zygote COW (shared pages excluded). Ceiling math: usable_renderers ≈ (RAM_budget − browser_RSS) / per_renderer_private_RSS.

budget 4 GiB, browser 60 MiB, per-renderer 3 MiB private
=> ~1340 renderers before OOM — so the process cap (≤256), NOT memory,
   is the practical bound for the stand-in. A real engine (50–150 MB/renderer)
   inverts this: memory binds first, cap is generous.

Design holds the cap as primary bound; memory pressure (below) tightens it dynamically.

IPC fan-out — the central scaling point

The browser process is the hub: all child IPC traverses sbx-browser's RouteId router + the single browser event loop. This is the structural bottleneck — every renderer↔browser, broker request, crash signal funnels through one process.

            renderers (R)        each: 1 MessagePipe (1 UDS fd)
   R1 R2 ... Rn   GPU   NET           N frames multiplexed by RouteId
     \  |  /  ___/  ___/              => fds scale O(processes), NOT O(frames)
      \ | / /     /
   +-----------------------+
   |  RouteId router (1)    |  <- central: demux by header.route, dispatch
   |  browser event loop(1) |  <- central: poll all pipes, run handlers
   +-----------------------+
  • RouteId multiplexing keeps it one pipe per process, not per frame -> fd count = O(processes) (≈ 4 + processes), not O(tabs/frames). Avoids fd exhaustion as tabs grow within a site.
  • Router cost per message = O(1) hashmap lookup RouteId -> handler. Throughput bound = single-loop syscall rate (recvmsg/sendmsg + epoll).

Throughput targets (steady-state, Linux primary): ≥ 200k small Messages/s aggregate through the router single-threaded; per-pipe ≥ 50k msg/s; broker request round-trip p99 < 200 µs. Large transfers bypass the router via sbx-shm ShmRing (zero-copy, SPSC) — keeps bulk pixel/buffer data off the message path.

Zygote: spawn + memory scalability

Zygote (Phase 6) is the spawn-scaling lever:

Metric Direct clone+exec Zygote fork
Spawn latency target ~10–30 ms < 3 ms p50, < 8 ms p99
Per-spawn page setup full exec, fresh pages COW from warmed template
Marginal RSS / renderer full private private = touched pages only (COW-shared runtime/libs)

COW sharing means M renderers share one warmed read-only page set -> aggregate RSS ≈ shared_once + M·private_delta, sublinear vs M fresh execs. Spawn is fork-bound, not exec/link-bound -> bursty tab opening (open 20 tabs) stays within budget. Single-fork-server serialization is acceptable: fork is sub-ms; if fork-server queueing appears under burst, mitigation = bounded request queue (not parallel servers — keeps the sealed template single-owner).

Memory-pressure handling

Under budget pressure (Phase 12), consolidate by site to shed per-process fixed overhead while preserving the strongest isolation possible:

on memory_pressure(level):
  level=Moderate -> lower process cap; route new sites to ReuseExistingForSite
  level=Critical -> proactively coalesce same-BrowsingInstance SiteInstances
                    onto one host; reclaim idle/background renderers first
  never coalesce across BrowsingInstances unless cap forces it (logged)

Reclaim order: idle background renderers > LRU same-site hosts > (last resort) cross-site coalescing. Each coalescing event is a measured isolation regression -> emitted as a metric, not silent.

Security-vs-resource tension (explicit)

This is the core trade-off and is stated as policy, not left implicit:

More processes Fewer processes
✅ smaller blast radius (per-SiteInstance CapabilitySet) ❌ a compromise spans co-resident sites
✅ true cross-site isolation ❌ Spectre-class cross-site read risk within a shared address space
❌ +fixed RSS, +fd, +process-table slot each ✅ lower fixed overhead, fits tight budgets

Invariant: the broker (PolicyEngine) re-validates every BrokerRequest regardless of process count — so consolidation degrades isolation granularity but never removes the second defense-in-depth layer. Reuse/coalescing is the only knob that trades security for resource; the seccomp + broker layers are never relaxed to scale.

Bottlenecks and mitigations

Bottleneck Scaling limit Mitigation
Single RouteId router + browser event loop one CPU's syscall/dispatch rate; HoL blocking across children shard router across worker threads (partition RouteId space by hash -> per-shard epoll loop); keep broker policy decisions serialized for auditability
Per-process fixed RSS RAM ceiling (real-engine case binds first) zygote COW; process cap; memory-pressure coalescing by site
fd table (per UDS pipe + brokered fds) RLIMIT_NOFILE in browser one pipe/process via RouteId mux; close brokered fds promptly post-pass; raise NOFILE in TCB only
Process-table / PID space system PID limit, cap clamp [16,256] hard process cap; ReuseExistingForSite at limit
Fork-server serialization burst spawn queueing bounded queue; fork is sub-ms so rarely binds
Broker request rate central PolicyEngine throughput O(1) capability lookup; cache ProcessId->CapabilitySet; brokered fd, not per-byte mediation

Net: the system scales to hundreds of isolated sites bounded by a hard process cap, with memory the true ceiling for a real engine; the single browser router/event loop is the central scaling risk, mitigated by RouteId pipe-multiplexing, ShmRing bulk offload, and (if needed) router sharding — all without ever weakening the two-layer (seccomp + broker) confinement that makes the model safe.

Mental Models & First Principles

The compressed insights a senior sandbox engineer holds before designing any system in this space. Each predicts a class of bugs you write without it; each resolves to a concrete section of this blueprint.

# Mental Model What It Predicts Bug Class Without It Applied In
MM1 A rendered web page is fully attacker-controlled code. The renderer process does not run "code you trusted" — it runs arbitrary JavaScript, native compiled code from a plugin, and parses untrusted network data. Threat model must start at "renderer is already compromised". Every design decision that says "but the renderer wouldn't do that" is wrong. Insufficient confinement justified by "we trust the content." §1.2, Threat Model T1, §6 P1
MM2 A Handle IS an authority; a path is a capability claim. Passing an FD via SCM_RIGHTS transfers a kernel-enforced capability — the receiving process can use whatever the FD refers to, regardless of filesystem namespace or credentials. Passing a path is passing a claim that must be verified, and the object can change between verification and use (TOCTOU). Any system that passes paths instead of FDs is TOCTOU-vulnerable. Broker validates a path at request time, but renderer opens it after the broker's check. §14 Phase 2, §19 Phase 7, ADR-06, P11
MM3 Defense-in-depth means one layer's bug does not yield escape. Two independent layers (seccomp filter AND broker policy) means an attacker must compromise both simultaneously. The seccomp filter blocks syscall-level primitives; the broker denies access to resources. A filter bypass that reaches the broker finds another deny; a broker logic bug that grants a resource finds seccomp has already blocked the syscall used to exploit it. Designing one strong layer gives false confidence. The invariant is "AND", not "OR". A seccomp filter bug in a single-layer design is a complete sandbox escape. §1.2, §6 P2, §7 INV-1, §17 Phase 5, §19 Phase 7
MM4 The seccomp filter is an allowlist, not a denylist. The BPF program's default action is KillProcess. Every syscall the renderer legitimately needs must be explicitly listed. A new kernel syscall is denied by default — not silently permitted. The arch-check must be the first instruction or the entire allowlist is bypassed via the x32/ia32 ABI. Forgetting one of these properties opens a bypass. A filter that blocks "bad" syscalls but allows the rest: the attacker finds a new syscall not on the denylist. §17 Phase 5, ADR-12, ADR-13, §7 INV-2
MM5 COW fork is a memory snapshot, not a copy. When the zygote forks a renderer, the child shares the parent's physical pages (marked read-only in the MMU). A write in the child triggers a page fault that copies only the touched page. The "cheap" renderer spawn is cheap because most of the process image (runtime, libraries, pre-warmed caches) is never written and therefore never copied. RSS growth per renderer equals the private dirty pages only. Assuming "fork is free regardless" leads to over-spawning and unexpected RSS growth. Forking a renderer that immediately writes a large buffer blows out COW savings; the zygote's value comes only from pre-warming the RO working set. §18 Phase 6, Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model BOE-1, B2 ch.9, ADR-17
MM6 Site isolation principal = eTLD+1, not full origin. The security principal is the registrable domain (scheme + eTLD+1 via the public suffix list), not the full origin (scheme+host+port). Two subdomains of the same eTLD+1 (e.g., a.example.com and b.example.com) are the same site and may share a process without Spectre risk — they can already read each other's cookies. Two domains on the same TLD but different eTLD+1 (e.g., a.github.io and b.github.io) are DIFFERENT sites and must be isolated, because github.io is a public suffix. Using naive "last two labels" as the site key misgroups a.co.uk and b.co.uk as the same site — a cross-site isolation bug. A naive label-counter without the PSL puts a.co.uk and b.co.uk in the same process, enabling cross-origin data leakage via Spectre. §21 Phase 9, ADR-18, ADR-19, P1, P9
MM7 The broker's job is to be the only path to the OS. A renderer in an empty NET namespace with pivot_root and full cap-drop cannot open(), connect(), or mmap() device memory directly — not because the broker says no, but because the kernel says the syscall would fail or is blocked by seccomp. The broker then validates the intent ("I need to read this file") against the renderer's CapabilitySet and returns only a pre-opened FD. This is object-capability security: authority is carried in the FD, not in the name. Thinking the broker is a "firewall" that blocks bad calls misses that the kernel already blocks them — the broker adds semantic validation on top. If the seccomp layer is misconfigured to allow open(2), the broker becomes the sole defense; if the broker is misconfigured, the seccomp layer becomes the sole defense. One can fail; both cannot simultaneously. §19 Phase 7, §20 Phase 8, §6 P3, MM3
MM8 Crash isolation = blast radius = CapabilitySet. A renderer crash (or full compromise) can cause at most what its CapabilitySet permits. If the CapabilitySet is {ReadFile(allowlist)}, the worst the attacker can do is read files on that allowlist. No capabilities that were not granted can be exercised. The browser process and sibling renderers are structurally unaffected — they hold separate address spaces and the broker validates against each caller's own ProcessId. Without explicit blast-radius reasoning, "compromise" is an unbounded bad outcome. A broker that does not isolate per-ProcessId allows a compromised renderer to impersonate another renderer and request its capabilities. §23 Phase 11, §20 Phase 8, §7 INV-14, Threat Model T1, P12
MM9 PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS is a one-way ratchet, not a filter. Once set, the process can never gain privilege via exec (SUID binaries become non-SUID, file capabilities are dropped). This is a precondition for unprivileged seccomp installation. The seccomp filter is then a capability over the syscall ABI; no_new_privs ensures that exec of a privileged binary does not escape the filter. The combination means the child is permanently capability-bounded regardless of what it execs. Assuming the seccomp filter alone is sufficient: if no_new_privs is absent, the filter survives exec but a SUID binary exec'd from inside the renderer would gain privilege before the filter acts on its syscalls. A renderer that can exec a SUID binary and escalate privilege; the filter is in place but the SUID gains privilege before the filter's syscall rules take effect on the elevated context. §17 Phase 5, §16 Phase 4, ADR-14, prctl(2), §7 INV-1
MM10 A hardware-enforced process boundary is a fundamentally stronger isolation primitive than a software-enforced one. The MMU page-table boundary between two processes is enforced by silicon the attacker cannot reprogram from user space; a software fault-isolation boundary (a WASM sandbox, a V8 isolate, an in-process interpreter) is enforced by code the same runtime executes, and a bug in that runtime (a JIT miscompile, a bounds-check elision) collapses the boundary from inside. This is why the design is multi-process rather than in-process-sandboxed. A single-process "sandbox" built from a memory-safe interpreter or a software fault-isolation scheme is not equivalent to an OS process boundary — it inherits every bug in its own enforcement layer. Treating a WASM/JS sandbox as sufficient confinement for fully-untrusted code and skipping the OS-level process boundary, then discovering a JIT or bounds-check bug breaks the "sandbox" with no second layer to catch it. §1.2, Alt-B (In-process Sandboxing — rejection rationale), P9 (Spectre — same-address-space isolation is unsafe), §6 P5

Comparative Architecture Table

Comparison of this design (project 266, sbx-*) against four production systems occupying the same space: process-level sandbox for untrusted content on a general-purpose OS. Rows = load-bearing architectural dimensions.

Dimension sbx-266 (this) Chromium (Linux) Firefox (Linux) gVisor (runsc) minijail
Language / TCB material Rust (memory-safe throughout TCB) C++ broker/launcher (memory-unsafe; dominant CVE class) C++ + Rust IPC (ipdl/Rust sandbox); mixed Go (GC runtime in sentry; memory-safe structurally) C (libminijail; memory-unsafe)
Syscall filtering Classic BPF (SyscallRule[] → cBPF); hand-built in SeccompFilter; arch-check first cBPF via bpf_dsl/; same arch-check; Chromium-maintained policy cBPF via SandboxBroker; separate content policy Sentry intercepts via PTRACE or KVM; gVisor handles the syscall, not seccomp cBPF via libseccomp; policy specified in .policy file; same arch-check
Namespace model USER + PID + NET + MNT + IPC + UTS; unprivileged via user-ns; pivot_root to minimal RO rootfs Same 6-ns set; historically SUID-sandbox + namespace; now namespace primary; pivot_root Same; unprivileged user-ns; pivot_root; content process in MNT/NET ns Container-level; gVisor's sentry runs in a separate kernel context (ptrace or KVM guest) intercepting all syscalls Namespace subset (USER, PID, MNT, NET, IPC, UTS configurable); pivot_root or chroot; rlimits
IPC / handle passing UNIX domain socket + SCM_RIGHTS; Interface/Endpoint<T>; RouteId mux; memfd ShmRing Mojo (Chromium's production IPC); message pipes, handle transfer, data pipes; platform-specific transport IPDL (Inter-Process Protocol Definition Language); message classes generated; MessageChannel gRPC-style within gVisor; host-sentry channel; sandbox-sentry channel (via shared memory or socket) No generic IPC — policy tool only; caller supplies IPC mechanism
Broker model PolicyEngine validates each BrokerRequest vs CapabilitySet; returns brokered FD only BrokerProcess per sandboxed process type; validates open/connect/socket; returns brokered FD SandboxBroker; validates file open/IPC per policy; returns fd Sentry IS the broker (full kernel emulation); no separate broker process Policy-only; no broker process; relies on seccomp to deny forbidden syscalls
Site isolation principal scheme + eTLD+1 via PSL → SiteInstance; process-per-site-instance Same — Chromium invented this (Reis et al. 2019); OOPIF; BrowsingInstance grouping Same since Firefox 54+ (multi-process) with Fission (FF 90+); full site isolation Not a browser — per-container isolation, not per-site Not applicable — not a browser
Spawn model Zygote fork-server (COW template → cheap fork); post-fork seccomp seal Zygote (Linux); direct launch (Windows/macOS); Chromium invented the zygote pattern Separate content process per tab or site; no zygote; preallocated pool in some modes runsc starts sentry process; forking within is sentry-managed Fork + exec or exec only; no zygote
Default disposition (seccomp) KillProcess (deny-by-default terminates) KillProcess for renderer; SIGSYS/TRAP for some audit paths KillProcess for content; Errno for some audit paths Seccomp in the outer sandbox layer kills on violation; sentry handles the inner layer Configurable; typically KILL or ERRNO depending on policy
Memory / resource budgets RLIMIT_AS/CPU/NOFILE per process type via ResourceBudget; memory-pressure consolidation rlimits + Chromium resource manager; OOM priority via oom_score_adj rlimits; about:memory for reporting; OOM score cgroup-based (container runtime provides limits) rlimits; configurable
Crash detection SIGCHLD + IPC-disconnect + exit watch; Minidump artifact; RestartPolicy CrashObserver / BrowserChildProcessObserver; Breakpad/Crashpad minidump ContentChild::crash_keys; Breakpad collector; sad tab Container restart policy (kubernetes / OCI runtime) Not applicable
Red-team / gate sbx attack harness; IPC fuzzer; seccomp conformance suite; gate in CI ClusterFuzz continuous fuzzer; security bugtracker; manual pen-test OSS-Fuzz; security bug bounty; IPC fuzz gVisor's runsc has its own compliance test suite Manual testing + seccomp-tools analysis

System Paragraphs

Chromium (Linux). Chromium invented the multi-process browser sandbox model in 2008 (P6) and the zygote pattern, and Chromium Linux sandbox serves as the primary reference implementation for this project. Where sbx-266 diverges: the TCB (broker, launcher, IPC parser) is in Rust, eliminating the C++ memory-unsafety CVE class that dominates Chromium's sandbox bug history. Chromium's breadth (Mojo IPC zoo, real renderer, multi-OS) is sbx-266's explicit non-goal; the minimal sbx-* design isolates only the security-critical subset. Chromium wins when shipping a real web engine with rendering, JS, media, and GPU support — the full Chromium complexity is justified by feature breadth, not avoidable. On security alone, Chromium's ongoing migration to Rust components (MojoBase, sandbox parts) is directionally converging toward sbx-266's approach.

Firefox (Linux). Firefox Fission (site isolation, shipped FF 90+) reaches the same principal model (scheme + eTLD+1, process per site) but on a C++/Rust mixed codebase with IPDL-generated IPC and the SandboxBroker. Firefox's sandbox layers (content process in MNT+NET+PID+USER ns + seccomp) are architecturally equivalent to sbx-266's Phases 4–5. Firefox wins in environments where the full browser is needed with strong site isolation and Mozilla's security track record; its mixed-language approach is a practical constraint of a large existing codebase, not a design choice to emulate.

gVisor (runsc). gVisor takes the fundamentally different approach of full syscall emulation: the sentry intercepts every syscall from the container process (via PTRACE or KVM guest mode) and implements a Linux-compatible kernel in Go. This gives a much stronger isolation guarantee (the host kernel's attack surface exposed to the container is tiny — only a handful of KVM or PTRACE syscalls). The tradeoff is 2–10× overhead on syscall-heavy workloads, a large sentry codebase, and Go's runtime incompatibility with direct fork/seccomp patterns. gVisor wins when the threat model requires a much larger reduction in host kernel attack surface than seccomp can provide — e.g., running fully untrusted third-party code where a kernel exploit in the sandboxed process is in scope. sbx-266 rejects this because the primary threat is a compromised web renderer (not a kernel exploit specialist), and seccomp + broker defense-in-depth plus site isolation is sufficient for that model while avoiding the 2–10× overhead.

minijail. minijail is a tool and library (C, from ChromiumOS) that applies namespace + seccomp + rlimit + pivot_root confinement to a process using a declarative policy file. It is the closest analog to sbx-266's confinement stack in isolation, but it is a policy-application tool, not a multi-process architecture: no broker, no IPC, no site isolation, no crash recovery. minijail is used inside Chrome's sandbox (it applies the seccomp policy to the browser helper processes on ChromeOS). sbx-266 studied minijail's policy DSL (P8, R4) to design SyscallPolicy and sbx-cli policy, but re-implements in safe Rust to remove the C memory-unsafety surface from the TCB. minijail wins when the deployment target is ChromeOS or a Linux embedded system where a battle-tested C tool is preferred over a Rust rewrite, and the workload is a single confined subprocess (not a multi-process browser architecture).

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

The six numbers a principal engineer computes before designing this system. Each is derived step-by-step from the §4 System Constraints table (C-1 through C-16). No number is asserted without derivation.

BOE-1 — Renderer private RSS per process (the memory floor)

Derivation from C-8 (per-renderer private RSS target: 2–4 MB for the minimal stand-in) and B2 (COW page semantics):

shared_pages = zygote image ≈ 30–60 MB (runtime + libs + warmed data, shared RO)
private_pages = touched-on-write pages per renderer ≈ 2–4 MB (C-8)
total_renderer_RSS = shared_pages (counted once) + N × private_pages
=> For N=20 renderers: 60 MB + 20 × 3 MB = 120 MB aggregate
=> Per-renderer marginal cost = 3 MB private (not 60 MB full image)
RLIMIT_AS for renderer = 512 MB (C-8 ceiling; headroom for growth)

This is why the zygote fork is the memory-scaling lever: 20 renderers cost 120 MB, not 20 × 63 MB = 1.26 GB.

BOE-2 — Renderer process count ceiling (memory-bounded)

Derivation from C-7 (total process budget) and BOE-1:

RAM budget (desktop) = 4 GB (reference machine, C-AV)
browser process RSS ≈ 60 MB (runtime + host registry + route tables)
renderer per-process marginal private = 3 MB (BOE-1)
available_for_renderers = 4096 MB − 60 MB = 4036 MB
renderer_count_ceiling = floor(4036 / 3) ≈ 1345 renderers
BUT: hard process cap C-7 = max_renderers = 2 × logical_cores, clamp [16, 256]
=> For 8-core machine: cap = min(16, 256) = 16 ... max(2×8=16, 256) = 16
=> Practical bound = process cap (16–256), NOT memory (1345)
=> Memory binds first only with a real engine (50–150 MB/renderer): 4036/100 = 40 renderers

BOE-3 — IPC message throughput (the router bottleneck)

Derivation from C-3 (IPC throughput target: ≥1 GB/s per pipe for 64 KB payloads; ≥200k small msgs/s aggregate) and C-2 (p50 round-trip < 20 µs):

small message (64 B payload + 24 B header = 88 B):
  sendmsg + recvmsg ≈ 2 syscalls at ~2 µs/syscall = 4 µs minimum
  => max single-pipe rate = 1 / 4 µs = 250k msg/s  [matches ≥200k aggregate target, C-3]
large message (64 KB payload):
  socket copy = 64 KB / memory bandwidth (≈50 GB/s DRAM) ≈ 1.3 µs copy
  + sendmsg overhead ≈ 2 µs
  => per-message ≈ 3.3 µs → ~300k msgs/s → ~300k × 64 KB = 19 GB/s theoretical
  BUT: socket buffer (default 128 KB–256 KB) limits in-flight data
  => bulk transfers move to ShmRing (zero-copy, SPSC, ≥5 GB/s target C-4)

BOE-4 — fd table budget in the browser process

Derivation from C-7 (max 256 renderers) and ADR-23 (one UDS pipe per process via RouteId mux):

fds per child = 1 (MessagePipe) + 1 (IPC with broker) = ~2
fds for 256 renderers = 256 × 2 = 512
+ GPU process: 1 fd
+ NET process: 1 fd
+ standard fds (stdin/stdout/stderr) = 3
+ internal pipes (reaper self-pipe, signal fd) ≈ 4
total ≈ 512 + 2 + 3 + 4 = ~521 fds in browser process
RLIMIT_NOFILE for browser = 4096 (C-16; headroom)
=> fd table headroom = 4096 − 521 = 3575 free slots
=> RouteId mux keeps this O(processes), not O(frames); critical for 10k+ tabs/site

BOE-5 — Zygote spawn latency (the fork bottleneck)

Derivation from C-5 (spawn latency target: zygote fork < 10 ms p50, < 20 ms p99) and B2 (COW fault cost):

fork(2) syscall overhead (Linux, 64-bit) ≈ 0.1–0.5 ms
COW page table copy (for 60 MB zygote image) ≈ 60 MB / 4 KB pages = 15360 PTEs
  PTE copy overhead ≈ 15360 × 20 ns/PTE ≈ 0.3 ms
post-fork seccomp seal (install BPF program) ≈ 0.1–0.5 ms
handshake (Hello/HelloAck RTT over loopback UDS) ≈ 0.1 ms
total p50 ≈ 0.5 + 0.3 + 0.3 + 0.1 = 1.2 ms  [well inside 10 ms target]
p99 headroom: +kernel scheduling jitter + COW fault burst on first dirty write
  ≈ 1.2 ms + 5 ms = 6.2 ms  [inside 20 ms p99 target]

BOE-6 — Broker request rate (the policy throughput)

Derivation from C-6 (broker request rate target: ≥10k requests/s under load) and ADR-20 (O(1) CapabilitySet lookup):

PolicyEngine: per-request = ProcessId → CapabilitySet lookup (HashMap) + op validation
  HashMap lookup ≈ 50–100 ns (L2/L3 cache hit on small map)
  op validation ≈ 50 ns (enum match + capability contains check)
  UDS round-trip (send request, recv FD) ≈ 4 µs (2 syscalls × 2 µs)
  => per-request wall time ≈ 4.1 µs → max rate ≈ 240k requests/s single-threaded
  => well above 10k/s target; bottleneck is UDS syscall cost, not PolicyEngine logic
capacity at 256 renderers, each issuing 40 broker requests/s:
  total = 256 × 40 = 10,240 requests/s ≈ at target; PolicyEngine never the bottleneck

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

Staff-level questions the finished reader must answer by synthesizing two or more sections. Each names the resolving §. No question is answerable from a single line.

  1. Why does the seccomp filter's arch-check instruction have to be FIRST, not anywhere in the BPF program? If it is not first, can an attacker reach syscalls that pass the filter by using a different ABI? Explain using the x32 example. Resolves: §17 Phase 5 (cBPF ordering), ADR-13, §7 INV-2, §5.4 struct seccomp_data.

  2. The broker validates every BrokerRequest, but what prevents a compromised renderer from impersonating a different renderer (e.g., the GPU process) to request GPU-level capabilities? Trace the exact validation path. Resolves: §19 Phase 7 (PolicyEngine per-ProcessId validation), §20 Phase 8 (CapabilitySet per ProcessId), §7 INV-11, ADR-20.

  3. If F_SEAL_WRITE is applied to a SharedBuffer before it is passed to the renderer, what exactly prevents the renderer from (a) resizing it, (b) writing to it, (c) remapping it writable? Reference the kernel mechanism and the ShmRing header position. Resolves: §14 Phase 2 (memfd seals), §11.3 (SharedBuffer sealing), ADR-09, memfd_create(2) §5.4.

  4. Why is ProcessReusePolicy::LimitReached an isolation regression, and what exactly is the blast-radius consequence of coalescing two different sites into one renderer? What metric is emitted? Resolves: §21 Phase 9 (ProcessReusePolicy), Scalability Analysis (security-vs-resource tension), §20 Phase 8 (CapabilitySet scope), Operational Readiness.

  5. A renderer in a PID namespace sees itself as PID 1. What happens when it calls fork(2) without the fork syscall being in the seccomp allow-list, and what happens if fork is in the allow-list? Connect the PID namespace semantics to the zygote design decision. Resolves: §17 Phase 5 (seccomp baseline renderer policy), §18 Phase 6 (zygote owns the fork path, renderers do not), ADR-12, §5.4 pid_namespaces(7).

  6. Why does pivot_root provide stronger isolation than chroot, and under what specific conditions can a chroot jail be escaped? Which exact sequences break chroot and why do they fail with pivot_root in a mount namespace? Resolves: §16 Phase 4 (pivot_root design), ADR-16, B5 (chroot pitfalls), §5.4 pivot_root(2).

  7. The ShmRing is SPSC with Acquire/Release ordering on the head and tail atomics. What breaks if you use Relaxed ordering? Give a concrete example of the memory reordering that would corrupt data. Resolves: §14 Phase 2 (ShmRing implementation), §11.3 (ShmRing ring semantics), §7 INV-4 (no-race on ring head/tail).

  8. Explain why a BrowsingInstance group of SiteInstances may share a process even if they are different sites (eTLD+1). When does site isolation override the BrowsingInstance grouping? Resolves: §21 Phase 9 (BrowsingInstance grouping rule), P1 (Reis et al. 2019 — BrowsingInstance + OOPIF), ADR-18.

  9. If PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS is set, and the renderer calls execve to re-exec itself as a SUID binary, what privilege does it obtain? Trace through no_new_privs semantics step by step. Resolves: §16 Phase 4 (no_new_privs ordering), §17 Phase 5 (install ordering), ADR-14, §5.4 prctl(2), MM9.

  10. The MessageHeader.num_handles field is validated against the SCM_RIGHTS fd count on recv. What are the two failure modes (mismatch in each direction) and what resource leak or security consequence does each create if not handled? Resolves: §11.2 (wire format, num_handles reconciliation), §14 Phase 2 (Handle recv validation), §7 INV-3, ADR-06.

  11. A renderer is killed with SIGKILL from outside the sandbox. Walk through all three crash-detection paths (SIGCHLD, IPC-disconnect, exit-watcher). Which fires first? Can any of them fail to fire? What if the browser process is also under memory pressure during the kill? Resolves: §23 Phase 11 (CrashHandler, three detection paths), §7 INV-6 (crash detected ≥1 path), Operational Readiness (SIGCHLD reaping).

  12. The SeccompFilter compiles SyscallRule[] to a cBPF program. If there are 200 allow-listed syscalls, approximately how many cBPF instructions does the emitted program contain, and does program length affect filter installation? What is the kernel's cBPF program-length limit? Resolves: §17 Phase 5 (cBPF compilation algorithm), §11.4 (SeccompFilter), ADR-11, §5.4 bpf(2) classic-BPF section.

  13. Why does the RouteId mux keep the browser process's fd count at O(processes) rather than O(frames)? What would the fd count be if there were one socket per RenderFrame, and at what point would that hit the process fd table limit? Resolves: Scalability Analysis (IPC fan-out), Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model BOE-4, ADR-23, §22 Phase 10 (router design).

  14. The zygote must warm COW pages before forking renderers. What exactly should it do to maximize page sharing — which pages SHOULD be pre-faulted and which MUST NOT be pre-faulted? Explain in terms of heap allocation, code pages, and thread-local state. Resolves: §18 Phase 6 (ForkServer COW warm pages), Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model BOE-1/BOE-5, B2 (COW semantics), ADR-17.

  15. If the PSL snapshot bundled in sbx-site/data/public_suffix_list.dat is six months old, and a new public suffix is added to the real PSL in the meantime, what is the security consequence? Is this better or worse than using a live DNS lookup? Resolves: §21 Phase 9 (PSL bundled snapshot rationale), ADR-19, P1 (site isolation cost data — stale PSL tradeoff), MM6.

  16. The PolicyEngine is described as an O(1) capability lookup. Walk through the exact data structure and the lookup algorithm. What is the CapabilitySet containment check and what happens if the check has a false-positive — i.e., it grants a capability the process should not have? Resolves: §20 Phase 8 (PolicyEngine, CapabilitySet), §11.6 (sbx-broker types), §7 INV-10, ADR-20, Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model BOE-6.

  17. What is the SECCOMP_FILTER_FLAG_TSYNC flag and should sbx-266 use it? What happens if a multi-threaded renderer is allowed to install a seccomp filter without TSYNC? Resolves: §17 Phase 5 (seccomp install), §5.4 seccomp(2), ADR-11/ADR-14.

  18. A renderer is in a NET namespace with no routable interface. The seccomp filter allows socket(AF_INET, ...). What happens when the renderer calls socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0) — does it succeed? If it does, can it connect() to the internet? Trace through the namespace and broker layers. Resolves: §16 Phase 4 (NET namespace isolation), §17 Phase 5 (seccomp allows socket but namespace denies connectivity), §19 Phase 7 (broker ConnectSocket validation), §6 P5, MM3.

  19. What is the BrokerOp OpenFile validation path for a request with a path containing ../etc/passwd? Trace from the renderer's request through PolicyEngine to the file system, step by step. Resolves: §19 Phase 7 (FileBroker path canonicalization, symlink prevention), P11 (Garfinkel TOCTOU), §7 INV-11, ADR-06.

  20. Why does ShmRing use a power-of-two capacity with bitmask indexing rather than modulo? In a SPSC ring, can the producer and consumer ever both be at the same index simultaneously? Prove it cannot happen. Resolves: §14 Phase 2 (ShmRing SPSC correctness), §11.3 (ring semantics), §7 INV-4.

  21. The sbx attack harness must verify that a forbidden syscall results in KillProcess. But KillProcess kills the child — how does the harness observe and assert the kill without itself crashing? Explain the test architecture. Resolves: §25 Phase 13 (attack harness architecture), §23 Phase 11 (crash detection), §7 INV-1/INV-2, ADR-26.

  22. Explain the difference between a SiteInstance and a BrowsingInstance. Can a single BrowsingInstance contain SiteInstances from different registrable domains? What is the consequence for process assignment? Resolves: §21 Phase 9 (SiteInstance/BrowsingInstance design), P2 (Chromium Site Isolation Design — BrowsingInstance scripting semantics), ADR-18.

  23. If the broker process itself crashes (e.g., due to a bug in PolicyEngine), what happens to all live renderers? Are they killed? Do they stall? Is the crash cascading? Describe the recovery path. Resolves: §23 Phase 11 (crash isolation — browser IS the broker; renderer crashes ≠ browser crashes), §7 INV-6, Failure Modes F1/F5.

  24. Why is SOCK_SEQPACKET used instead of SOCK_STREAM for MessagePipe? What would break about the framing and handle-passing protocol if SOCK_STREAM were used instead? Resolves: §13 Phase 1 (SOCK_SEQPACKET boundary semantics), §14 Phase 2 (SCM_RIGHTS atomicity requirement), §11.2 (wire format), ADR-05.

  25. The ProcessReusePolicy::LimitReached path puts two different sites in one renderer. How does the two-layer (seccomp + broker) defense-in-depth continue to hold in this degraded state, even though site isolation is weakened? Resolves: §21 Phase 9 (LimitReached reuse), Scalability Analysis (security-vs-resource tension), §20 Phase 8 (CapabilitySet still applies per-ProcessId), MM3, MM7.

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Four fundamentally different architectures for the same system goal (process-level browser sandbox), each with a sketch, the regime where it wins, the real system that embodies it, and why sbx-266 rejected it.

Alt-A: Full Syscall Emulation (gVisor-style sentry)

+---------------------------+
|  Renderer (untrusted)     |
|  syscall: open("x")       |
+------ PTRACE / KVM -------+
|  SENTRY (Go userspace     |  <- intercepts ALL syscalls
|  kernel emulation)        |  <- implements Linux ABI in Go
+------ host kernel ---------+
|  Host kernel              |
|  (tiny exposed surface)   |
+---------------------------+

Regime where it wins: When the threat model includes kernel exploits from a compromised renderer — i.e., a sophisticated attacker who can exploit a kernel vulnerability reachable through the syscall ABI. The sentry's virtualization approach means the host kernel sees only a handful of hypervisor/PTRACE calls, dramatically reducing the attack surface compared to 400+ seccomp-filtered syscalls. gVisor is used in Google Cloud Run and Cloud Functions where multi-tenant workloads require container isolation stronger than seccomp alone.

Real system embodying it: gVisor (runsc, Google, open-source 2018).

Why sbx-266 rejected it: 2–10× syscall overhead (every renderer syscall traps into the sentry) is unacceptable for a browser that calls read/write/mmap millions of times per second during rendering. The sentry's Go runtime is incompatible with direct fork/seccomp/namespace patterns, requiring a completely different process model. The threat model (compromised web renderer, not kernel exploit specialist) does not require kernel-exploit-level isolation. seccomp + broker defense-in-depth is sufficient for the renderer threat model and avoids the 2–10× overhead.

Alt-B: In-process Sandboxing (NaCl / WebAssembly / SFI-style)

+--------------------------------------------------+
|  Browser process                                 |
|  +--------------------------------------------+  |
|  |  Renderer (in-process sandbox)             |  |
|  |  WASM / SFI / NaCl: software fault isolation|  |
|  |  address space partitioned by SW checks    |  |
|  +--------------------------------------------+  |
|  IPC: function calls / shared memory           |  |
+--------------------------------------------------+

Regime where it wins: When spawn latency, IPC latency, or inter-renderer shared state make process-level isolation too expensive — e.g., real-time collaborative editing tools or game engines where cross-component latency must be microseconds. WebAssembly sandboxing (WASM + Wasmtime or V8 WASM) is O(ns) for cross-sandbox calls vs O(µs) for UDS IPC; for workloads where 100k sandbox crossings per second are required, in-process SFI wins.

Real system embodying it: Google Native Client (NaCl, 2008); WebAssembly (W3C, 2019+); Lucet/Wasmtime (Fastly, Bytecode Alliance).

Why sbx-266 rejected it: In-process isolation cannot provide true address-space separation — a Spectre-class speculative read can cross the software-enforced boundary regardless of software checks, because the CPU speculatively executes outside the checked bounds. After Spectre/Meltdown (2018, P9), in-process isolation for cross-origin web content is architecturally broken; the fix is process-level separation. NaCl was retired from Chrome in 2019 precisely because WASM + process isolation is more correct. sbx-266 exists to implement the correct model.

Alt-C: Language-Runtime Isolation (Deno/QuickJS-in-subprocess style)

+----------------------------------------------------------+
|  Browser process                                         |
|  sbx-browser: hosts multiple renderer "isolates"        |
|  +------------------------+ +-------------------------+  |
|  |  Renderer isolate A    | |  Renderer isolate B     |  |
|  |  Deno/V8 isolate;      | |  (same process)         |  |
|  |  no OS process boundary| |                         |  |
|  +------------------------+ +-------------------------+  |
|  IPC: in-process message passing (no UDS)               |
+----------------------------------------------------------+

Regime where it wins: When the renderer runs a scripting language (JavaScript/TypeScript) whose runtime provides strong in-language isolation guarantees — e.g., Deno isolates, Cloudflare Workers (V8 isolates per request). Each V8 isolate has its own heap and cannot access another's memory via the V8 API. For workloads where the entire renderer is a managed language runtime with no native code execution, language-level isolation is cheap (microseconds) and effectively correct (assuming a correct V8). Cloudflare Workers serves millions of isolates per machine this way.

Real system embodying it: Deno (Ryan Dahl, 2018); Cloudflare Workers (V8 isolates, 2017); Node.js VM module.

Why sbx-266 rejected it: sbx-266's renderer is explicitly a general-purpose process, not a language sandbox. The renderer runs native C/Rust code (layout engine, image decoder, media) with arbitrary syscall access. A language runtime cannot sandbox native code — a JIT-compiled escape or a native plugin would bypass the isolate boundary. More fundamentally, this architecture requires trust in the language runtime's isolation (V8 is the dominant browser CVE class), whereas sbx-266 requires trust only in the kernel and the TCB — a much smaller and more auditable set.

Alt-D: Microkernel / Capability OS (seL4-style process model)

+--------------------------------------------------------------+
|  Browser "application"                                       |
|  +---------------------+   +----------------------------+   |
|  |  Renderer server    |   |  File server (FS cap)      |   |
|  |  holds only IPC cap |   |  validates file access cap |   |
|  +---------------------+   +----------------------------+   |
|  seL4 / MINIX3: all IPC via capability endpoint calls       |
|  kernel = 10,000 lines, formally verified                   |
+--------------------------------------------------------------+

Regime where it wins: When formal verification of the entire isolation stack is required — safety-critical or high-assurance environments (avionics, medical devices, military systems) where a CVE in the OS kernel is an unacceptable risk. seL4 is formally verified (no implementation bugs at the kernel level), the microkernel has a minimal attack surface (10k lines), and the capability model is enforced by hardware. The DARPA HACMS project used seL4 for unmanned vehicle isolation with this rationale.

Real system embodying it: seL4 (CSIRO/Data61, 2009); MINIX3; Genode OS framework.

Why sbx-266 rejected it: Linux is the deployment target — no existing Linux process can run on seL4 without a complete port. The Chromium browser codebase, the GPU drivers, and the userland libraries sbx-266 depends on do not run on seL4. Even if they did, the engineering cost of a microkernel port for a browser sandbox exceeds the security gain over the existing two-layer (seccomp + broker) model for the web-browser threat model. sbx-266 achieves a practically sound security posture on a commodity OS (Linux) — the security bar of seL4 is warranted for embedded safety-critical systems, not for a desktop browser where the development velocity on Linux dominates.

Deferred Features Register

Every PhaseDeferred(...) used anywhere in this document, with the trigger that un-defers it. Anchors resolve to real headings in the Phase-by-Phase Plan.

Feature PhaseDeferred anchor Enabling phase Un-defer trigger
macOS Seatbelt backend (Sandbox impl for macOS) PhaseDeferred("§3 sbx-sandbox macos_stub.rs") After Phase 5 (## 17) Decision to ship on macOS; implement Seatbelt::apply in sbx-sandbox/src/macos_stub.rs; must pass equivalent of the Phase 5 red-team harness on macOS.
Windows (job object / restricted token / AppContainer) backend PhaseDeferred("§3 sbx-sandbox windows_stub.rs") After Phase 5 (## 17) Decision to ship on Windows; implement WindowsSandbox::apply in sbx-sandbox/src/windows_stub.rs; requires Windows-privileged CI runner.
GPU / Vulkan channel (real GPU command stream) PhaseDeferred("§1.3 GPU channel") After Phase 8 (## 20) Decision to connect a real GPU backend; requires modeling the relaxed-sandbox GPU process channel, DRM FD passing, and capability extension (GpuChannel cap).
Real network-service brokering (full TCP/TLS/HTTP) PhaseDeferred("§1.3 network stack") After Phase 7 (## 19) Project #33/#64 (network stack) reaches a stable API; wire SocketBroker to real TCP/TLS instead of the stub-only ConnectSocket path.
Real renderer (layout engine, JS, media) PhaseDeferred("§1.3 real renderer") After all 14 phases Project #260/#305 (rendering engine) matures; replace sbx-renderer stand-in with the real engine running inside the sandbox.
SECCOMP_USER_NOTIF (user-notify) for dynamic brokering PhaseDeferred("§17 Phase 5 seccomp user-notify") After Phase 5 (## 17) Requirement for per-syscall-argument brokering without Trap+signal cost; implement SECCOMP_RET_USER_NOTIF path in sbx-sandbox.
sbx spawn / sbx policy / sbx attack CLI completeness PhaseDeferred("§12 Phase 0 CLI") Phase 0–13 (## 12–## 25) Each subcommand un-defers as its backing subsystem is complete (see exit-2 contract in Phase 0).
Storage process type (ProcessType::Storage) PhaseDeferred("§1.6 sbx-core ProcessType") After Phase 8 (## 20) Decision to add a storage isolation boundary; define Capability::ReadDB/WriteDB; extend PolicyEngine profiles.

Glossary

Terms are defined precisely and implementation-relevantly. Crate names are sbx-*; the binary is sbx. Linux is the primary target.

Term Definition
browser process The single privileged orchestrator (sbx-browser::BrowserProcess, ProcessType::Browser, ProcessRole::Privileged). The TCB. Owns ProcessHost, launcher, brokers, site policy, crash handler, IPC router. Never untrusted input executes here unsandboxed.
renderer process Fully untrusted child (sbx-renderer::RendererProcess, ProcessType::Renderer, ProcessRole::Sandboxed). Confined by seccomp-bpf + namespaces; holds only its CapabilitySet; reaches OS resources only via the broker. A minimal stand-in here — not a web engine.
GPU process Child (ProcessType::Gpu) with a relaxed sandbox (needs device/driver access); holds a GpuChannel capability. Isolates the graphics driver attack surface from the browser.
network process Child (ProcessType::Network) holding ConnectSocket capabilities; the only child permitted (brokered) network egress, since renderers run in a NET namespace with no network.
utility process Child (ProcessType::Utility) for one-off untrusted work (parsing, decoding) with a minimal CapabilitySet; short-lived, tightest sandbox.
broker A privileged service in the browser (sbx-broker: FileBroker/SocketBroker/ResourceBroker, Broker trait) that performs OS operations a sandboxed child cannot, after PolicyEngine validation. Returns a brokered FD via SCM_RIGHTS, never a path.
sandbox OS-level confinement of a child (sbx-sandbox: Sandbox trait, LinuxSandbox, Policy). Combines namespaces + pivot_root + rlimits + cap drop + no_new_privs + seccomp-bpf.
seccomp-bpf Kernel syscall-filtering mechanism: a classic-BPF program (SeccompFilter) attached to a process that the kernel runs on every syscall, returning a disposition. Installed via prctl(PR_SET_SECCOMP, SECCOMP_MODE_FILTER) after no_new_privs.
syscall filter The SeccompFilter cBPF program compiled from SyscallPolicy (Vec<SyscallRule>); per-syscall nr matched, optional ArgFilter, then a Disposition. One of the two defense layers.
disposition sbx-sandbox::Disposition — the verdict for a filtered syscall: Allow, Errno(i32), Trap (→ SIGSYS), KillProcess, Log. Maps to SECCOMP_RET_ALLOW/ERRNO/TRAP/KILL_PROCESS/LOG.
seccomp_data The kernel struct the cBPF program reads: { nr: i32, arch: u32, instruction_pointer: u64, args: [u64; 6] }. arch MUST be checked first to reject non-AUDIT_ARCH_* ABIs (blocks the x32/ia32 syscall-number bypass).
namespace Linux kernel isolation of a global resource (sbx-sandbox::Namespaces, clone flags). Six used: user (maps child to fake root, enables the rest unprivileged), pid (hides other processes), net (removes all network → forces brokering), mnt (private mounts for pivot_root), ipc (isolates SysV IPC), uts (isolates hostname).
pivot_root pivot_root(2) syscall that swaps the child's root mount to a minimal, mostly read-only rootfs (Policy::root); strips access to the real filesystem tree. Done inside the mnt namespace.
no_new_privs prctl(PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS, 1) — irrevocably bars the process (and descendants) from gaining privileges via setuid/setgid/fcaps. Prerequisite for an unprivileged process to install a seccomp filter.
rlimit Per-process resource ceiling (Policy::rlimits → Rlimits): RLIMIT_AS/NOFILE/NPROC/CPU/CORE etc. Caps blast radius (memory/FD/fork exhaustion) and disables core dumps.
capability sbx-core::Capability — a single least-privilege grant a child may exercise (OpenFile{path_glob,mode}, ConnectSocket{host,port}, SharedMemory{max_bytes}, ReadClipboard, GpuChannel). Distinct from a Linux POSIX capability (which is dropped).
CapabilitySet sbx-core::CapabilitySet — the per-process set of granted Capabilitys. The PolicyEngine validates every BrokerRequest against the caller's set; it is the precise statement of a compromised child's reach.
least privilege Design rule: each ProcessType gets the minimum CapabilitySet to function (renderer minimal; network/GPU relaxed). Narrows what a sandbox escape can do.
deny by default Broker + seccomp posture: any syscall/resource not explicitly allowed is denied (Errno/KillProcess / BrokerResponse::Denied). New code fails closed, not open.
zygote sbx-zygote::Zygote — a pre-forked, pre-sandboxed template process holding warmed read-only pages; renderers spawn as cheap COW forks from it instead of fresh execs.
fork server sbx-zygote::ForkServer — services ForkRequest{process_type, policy, channel} over a socket by fork()ing the zygote and sealing the child's seccomp policy before untrusted work runs.
copy-on-write (COW) Kernel page-sharing after fork(): child and zygote share identical pages read-only until written. Makes zygote spawn fast and memory-cheap (shared runtime/libs).
IPC Inter-process communication (sbx-ipc). All cross-process traffic over UNIX domain sockets; the only sanctioned channel across the trust boundary; carries Messages, FDs (SCM_RIGHTS), and shared-memory handles.
message pipe sbx-ipc::MessagePipe — a bidirectional UDS-backed channel (pair() = socketpair); the transport for Message{header, payload, handles}.
Mojo The Chromium typed-IPC system this layer is modeled on: typed Interfaces bound to message-pipe endpoints with request/response + async. sbx-ipc is "Mojo-like."
Interface sbx-ipc::Interface trait — a typed request/response contract bound to an Endpoint; gives compile-time-shaped messages instead of raw bytes.
Endpoint sbx-ipc::Endpoint<T: Interface> — a typed end of a message pipe carrying interface T; the RouteId-addressed binding point.
SCM_RIGHTS UNIX-socket ancillary message (unix(7)) that duplicates an open FD into the receiver. The capability-passing primitive: the broker passes an opened FD, never a path → eliminates path-reinterpretation TOCTOU.
handle passing Moving an OS handle across a process boundary via SCM_RIGHTS as a sbx-ipc::Handle (owned FD with close-on-drop). MessageHeader::num_handles declares count; receiver takes ownership.
shared memory sbx-shm — large/zero-copy data shared between processes via a memfd FD passed over IPC, then mmaped (SharedBuffer, Mapping). Avoids byte-copying big payloads through the socket.
memfd Anonymous file from memfd_create(2) backing a SharedBuffer; supports seals; the FD is the shareable handle.
seal fcntl F_SEAL_* flag on a memfd (F_SEAL_WRITE/SHRINK/GROW/SEAL) making the region immutable/fixed-size, so an untrusted peer cannot resize or rewrite shared memory underneath the broker.
ShmRing sbx-shm::ShmRing — a single-producer single-consumer ring buffer over a SharedBuffer for streaming large transfers without per-message IPC overhead.
site isolation The policy that each site runs in its own process (sbx-site), so a compromised renderer cannot read another site's data in-process.
SiteInstance sbx-site::SiteInstance{id, site: SiteId, browsing_instance} — a specific site within a specific BrowsingInstance; the principal for process assignment + capability grants.
BrowsingInstance sbx-site::BrowsingInstance{id, instances} — a group of SiteInstances that may script each other (e.g., via window references) and therefore may share a process.
eTLD+1 "Effective top-level domain plus one label" — the registrable domain (e.g. foo.co.uk); the site key together with scheme. Computed via the public suffix list.
registrable domain Synonym for eTLD+1: the highest domain a single party can register; the unit of same-site grouping.
public suffix list The dataset of effective TLDs (co.uk, github.io, …) used by SiteIsolationPolicy::site_for to derive eTLD+1 correctly; without it, attacker-controlled subdomains could be mis-grouped.
process-per-site-instance ProcessReusePolicy::ProcessPerSiteInstance — the default: one process per SiteInstance, subject to a process-count limit.
process reuse sbx-site::ProcessReusePolicy (ReuseExistingForSite, LimitReached) — when the process limit/memory pressure is hit, reuse an existing same-site process rather than spawn anew.
RouteId sbx-core::RouteId(u32) — multiplexing key in MessageHeader::route; lets many frames/interfaces share one MessagePipe. The sbx-browser router dispatches by RouteId.
trust boundary The line between the privileged browser/broker and the untrusted children (drawn in the architecture diagram). Everything crossing it (IPC messages, brokered FDs) is treated as hostile and validated.
TCB (Trusted Computing Base) The code that must be correct for the security model to hold: the browser process + brokers + PolicyEngine + sandbox setup. Minimized + memory-safe (Rust) by design; renderers are explicitly outside it.
defense in depth Two independent confinement layers — the seccomp syscall filter AND the broker policy — so a single layer's bug does not yield escape.
blast radius The maximal damage from a compromised child: exactly its CapabilitySet, since the broker re-validates every request. Bounded, not zero.
sad tab The user-visible signal (sbx-crash) that a renderer died; the browser + other renderers stay alive (renderer crash ≠ browser crash).
minidump sbx-crash::Minidump — a minimal crash artifact (pid, signal, registers-ish, maps snapshot) captured on child death for post-mortem; not a full core dump.
restart policy sbx-crash::RestartPolicy (Never, Restart{max, window_secs}, ReloadTab) governing whether/how a dead child is respawned; rate-limited to avoid crash loops.
sandbox escape An exploit that breaks a child out of its OS confinement (seccomp/namespaces). The seccomp arch check, deny-by-default policy, and minimal kernel surface are the mitigations the red-team harness attacks.
privilege escalation A compromised child gaining rights beyond its CapabilitySet (e.g., tricking the broker). Prevented by per-ProcessId capability validation on every BrokerRequest.
confused deputy An attack where the renderer induces the privileged broker to misuse its authority on the renderer's behalf. Countered by validating each BrokerOp against the caller's CapabilitySet (caller identity bound to the connection, not asserted in the message).
brokered resource An OS resource (file/socket/shm) the renderer cannot obtain directly; the broker opens it after policy validation and passes back only the resulting Handle via SCM_RIGHTS.

Appendix A — File Inventory

Every leaf file from §10 Repo Layout. Linux is the primary target; macOS/Windows sandbox backends are PhaseDeferred stubs. One purpose line per file. Workspace = 12 sbx-* members + governance + tests + tooling.

A.1 Workspace root & governance

File Purpose
Cargo.toml Virtual workspace manifest; members = [12 sbx-*]; pinned deps (nix, libc); shared profiles.
Cargo.lock Locked dependency graph; committed (TCB reproducibility).
rust-toolchain.toml MSRV 1.78, edition 2021; pins toolchain for seccomp/clone determinism.
Makefile build/test/lint/fuzz/bench/attack/docker targets; single entry for CI.
Dockerfile Linux image with kernel headers + rootfs skeleton; runs sandbox tests under required caps.
docker-compose.yml Services: build · test · fuzz · bench · attack-harness · privileged-kernel test runner.

clippy.toml Lint thresholds; denies unwrap in TCB crates.

.github/workflows/fuzz.yml Scheduled short fuzz runs of the four targets; uploads crash corpora.

.github/workflows/release.yml Tag-driven build; SBOM; checksums; dual-license bundle.

A.2 sbx-core — shared OS-free types (no IPC, no syscalls)

File Purpose
sbx-core/Cargo.toml Manifest; zero OS deps.
sbx-core/src/lib.rs Crate root; re-exports ids/process/capability/error.
sbx-core/src/ids.rs ProcessId, RouteId, HandleId, SiteId, BrowsingInstanceId newtypes.
sbx-core/src/process_type.rs ProcessType, ProcessRole (Privileged/Sandboxed).
sbx-core/src/capability.rs Capability, CapabilitySet, AccessMode; least-privilege grant model.
sbx-core/src/error.rs SbxError (Ipc/Sandbox/PolicyDenied/SpawnFailed/ChildCrashed/BadHandle).
sbx-core/tests/types.rs Capability subset/containment + id invariants.

A.3 sbx-ipc — Mojo-like IPC over UNIX domain sockets

File Purpose
sbx-ipc/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-core, nix.
sbx-ipc/src/lib.rs Crate root; exports pipe/message/wire/interface/handle.
sbx-ipc/src/pipe.rs MessagePipe (socketpair UDS), pair(), send/recv loop.
sbx-ipc/src/message.rs Message, MessageHeader, MsgKind; header field layout.
sbx-ipc/src/framing.rs Framing + serialization (length-prefixed); bounds checks; malformed-input rejection.
sbx-ipc/src/handle.rs Handle (owned fd); SCM_RIGHTS send/recv; close-on-drop, dup, ownership transfer.
sbx-ipc/src/endpoint.rs Endpoint<T: Interface>; typed message-pipe end; Sender/Receiver wiring.
sbx-ipc/src/interface.rs Interface trait; request/response + async contract + req_id correlation; route binding.
sbx-ipc/tests/roundtrip.rs Pipe pair message roundtrip; req/response matching.
sbx-ipc/tests/handle_passing.rs FD passed via SCM_RIGHTS is usable in receiver; refcount/close semantics.

A.4 sbx-shm — shared memory (memfd-backed)

File Purpose
sbx-shm/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-core, sbx-ipc, nix.
sbx-shm/src/lib.rs Crate root; exports buffer/mapping/ring.
sbx-shm/src/buffer.rs SharedBuffer via memfd_create; Prot; seal flags (F_SEAL_*).
sbx-shm/src/mapping.rs Mapping (mmap ro/rw, Deref to bytes); unmap-on-drop.
sbx-shm/src/ring.rs ShmRing SPSC ring for large zero-copy transfers; head/tail indices.
sbx-shm/tests/seal.rs Sealed buffer rejects resize/write; ro mapping faults on write.
sbx-shm/tests/ring.rs SPSC ring producer/consumer correctness, wraparound.

A.5 sbx-sandbox — OS confinement (Linux primary)

File Purpose
sbx-sandbox/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-core, libc, nix.
sbx-sandbox/src/lib.rs Sandbox trait; backend selection; Policy, Disposition, SyscallRule.
sbx-sandbox/src/policy.rs Policy struct; Rlimits; assembly of namespaces+seccomp+root+caps.
sbx-sandbox/src/seccomp.rs SeccompFilter: compiles SyscallRule[] -> cBPF over seccomp_data; arch-first check (block x32/ia32).
sbx-sandbox/src/syscall_policy.rs SyscallPolicy DSL; ArgFilter; baseline renderer allow-list.
sbx-sandbox/src/namespaces.rs Namespaces clone flags USER/PID/NET/MNT/IPC/UTS; uid/gid map writing.
sbx-sandbox/src/fs.rs pivot_root/chroot to minimal rootfs; remount ro; tmpfs setup.
sbx-sandbox/src/caps.rs Drop all capabilities; PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS; rlimit application.
sbx-sandbox/src/linux.rs LinuxSandbox impl: orders namespaces -> fs -> caps -> no_new_privs -> seccomp.
sbx-sandbox/src/macos_stub.rs PhaseDeferred Seatbelt stub.
sbx-sandbox/src/windows_stub.rs PhaseDeferred job-object/restricted-token/AppContainer stub.
sbx-sandbox/tests/seccomp_compile.rs cBPF program shape; disposition encoding; arch gate present.
sbx-sandbox/tests/namespaces.rs User-ns mapping + child sees isolated pid/net/mnt (privileged).

A.6 sbx-zygote — pre-forked, pre-sandboxed template

File Purpose
sbx-zygote/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-sandbox, sbx-ipc.
sbx-zygote/src/lib.rs Crate root; exports zygote/fork_server/protocol.
sbx-zygote/src/zygote.rs Zygote: fork-server socket + base policy; warms COW read-only pages.
sbx-zygote/src/fork_server.rs ForkServer: services ForkRequest via fork() + post-fork seal.
sbx-zygote/src/protocol.rs ForkRequest; request/reply wire format over the zygote channel.
sbx-zygote/tests/fork.rs Forked child inherits base sandbox, applies tightened policy, runs.

A.7 sbx-broker — privileged resource broker

File Purpose
sbx-broker/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-ipc, sbx-core.
sbx-broker/src/lib.rs Broker trait; BrokerRequest, BrokerResponse, BrokerOp.
sbx-broker/src/broker.rs ResourceBroker dispatch: routes BrokerOp -> File/Socket/Resource broker; audit log of decisions.
sbx-broker/src/file_broker.rs FileBroker: validated OpenFile; returns fd, never path.
sbx-broker/src/socket_broker.rs SocketBroker: validated ConnectSocket; returns connected fd.
sbx-broker/src/resource.rs ResourceBroker: shm/clipboard/gpu-channel ops.
sbx-broker/src/policy_engine.rs PolicyEngine: ProcessId -> CapabilitySet; per-op validation; deny-by-default.
sbx-broker/tests/deny_by_default.rs Unlisted op denied; capability mismatch denied.
sbx-broker/tests/brokered_fd.rs Allowed OpenFile yields working fd over IPC.

A.8 sbx-launcher — process spawning + lifecycle

File Purpose
sbx-launcher/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-sandbox, sbx-ipc, sbx-zygote.
sbx-launcher/src/lib.rs ProcessLauncher, LaunchOptions, ChildProcess, ProcessHandle.
sbx-launcher/src/launcher.rs clone/exec or zygote path (use_zygote); channel + args + env setup.
sbx-launcher/src/handshake.rs Post-spawn hello handshake; readiness ack.
sbx-launcher/src/child.rs SIGCHLD reap; restart hooks; ProcessHandle state.
sbx-launcher/tests/launch.rs Direct + zygote launch; handshake completes; clean exit reaped.

A.9 sbx-site — site-isolation policy

File Purpose
sbx-site/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-core.
sbx-site/src/lib.rs Crate root; exports instance/policy/psl.
sbx-site/src/site_instance.rs SiteInstance{id,site,browsing_instance}.
sbx-site/src/browsing_instance.rs BrowsingInstance{id,instances}; same-instance SiteInstances may script (share process).
sbx-site/src/policy.rs SiteIsolationPolicy, ProcessReusePolicy; site->process map; process-count limit.
sbx-site/src/psl.rs Public-suffix list; eTLD+1 / registrable-domain derivation.
sbx-site/data/public_suffix_list.dat Embedded PSL data fixture.
sbx-site/tests/etld.rs eTLD+1 cases (multi-level suffixes, IP, localhost); site_for correctness.
sbx-site/tests/reuse.rs Process-per-site-instance vs reuse vs limit-reached transitions.

A.10 sbx-browser — browser (broker) orchestrator [TCB]

File Purpose
sbx-browser/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps launcher/ipc/broker/site/crash.
sbx-browser/src/lib.rs BrowserProcess; owns hosts, launcher, brokers, site policy, crash handler, router; re-exports.
sbx-browser/src/browser.rs BrowserProcess event loop; navigation->SiteInstance->process assignment; wires all subsystems.
sbx-browser/src/process_host.rs ProcessHost registry keyed by ProcessId.
sbx-browser/src/render_process_host.rs RenderProcessHost: pid + SiteInstance + routes.
sbx-browser/src/router.rs RouteId IPC router; multiplex many frames over one pipe.
sbx-browser/src/navigation.rs Navigator::navigate(url)->RouteId; the assign-or-reuse algorithm.
sbx-browser/src/metrics.rs Metrics{registry}; counters/gauges/histograms exposed to Phase 12 observability (§28.1).
sbx-browser/src/trace.rs TraceId/SpanId/Span; cross-boundary trace-id propagation in MessageHeader.
sbx-browser/tests/navigation.rs Same-site reuse, cross-site new process, route demux.

A.11 sbx-renderer — minimal untrusted renderer child

File Purpose
sbx-renderer/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-ipc, sbx-sandbox.
sbx-renderer/src/main.rs Renderer binary entry: enter sandbox, serve IPC, host RenderFrames.
sbx-renderer/src/lib.rs RendererProcess lib surface; re-exports.
sbx-renderer/src/renderer.rs Main loop: accepts navigation, requests resources via broker, seals sandbox.
sbx-renderer/src/frame.rs RenderFrame (route + url); broker resource requests.
sbx-renderer/src/attack_payload.rs cfg(attack) in-child red-team probes (Phase 13): raw syscalls, traversal BrokerOp, malformed Message; reports outcome over IPC.
sbx-renderer/tests/sandboxed.rs Renderer runs under policy; brokered open succeeds, direct open denied.

A.12 sbx-crash — crash handling + recovery

File Purpose
sbx-crash/Cargo.toml Manifest; deps sbx-core, sbx-ipc.
sbx-crash/src/lib.rs Crate root; exports handler/minidump/restart.
sbx-crash/src/handler.rs CrashHandler: SIGCHLD/exit/IPC-disconnect watcher; blast-radius isolation.
sbx-crash/src/minidump.rs Minidump: pid, signal, registers-ish, maps snapshot.
sbx-crash/src/restart.rs RestartPolicy (Never/Restart{max,window}/ReloadTab); "sad tab" signal.
sbx-crash/tests/restart.rs Crash detected; restart within window; over-limit -> sad tab.

A.13 sbx-cli — the sbx binary

sbx-cli/src/cmd/run.rs run: full browser + child orchestration.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/spawn.rs spawn: launch one typed child.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/policy.rs policy: dump/inspect seccomp + capability profiles.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/ipc_ping.rs ipc-ping: IPC roundtrip / latency probe.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/sandbox_test.rs sandbox-test: apply policy + assert confinement.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/crash_test.rs crash-test: force child crash + observe recovery.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/sites.rs sites: eTLD+1 derivation + process-map view.
sbx-cli/src/cmd/attack.rs attack: red-team harness entry.

File Purpose

conformance/attack/forbidden_sockets.toml Direct connect attempts (must be brokered) → expect ENOSYS/deny.
conformance/attack/arch_bypass.toml x32/ia32 ABI bypass attempts → filter MUST reject (arch check first).

fuzz/fuzz_targets/framing.rs Fuzz wire framing decode (length-prefix/bounds); malformed → error, not crash.
fuzz/fuzz_targets/broker_request.rs Fuzz BrokerRequest/BrokerOp parsing + validation.
fuzz/fuzz_targets/seccomp_builder.rs Fuzz SyscallRule[] -> cBPF compilation (no panic/overflow).
fuzz/fuzz_targets/psl.rs Fuzz PSL parsing + site_for URL inputs.

scripts/syscall-table.sh Generate per-arch syscall nr tables for seccomp.rs.
scripts/check-arch.sh Verify AUDIT_ARCH_* constants match target (arch-bypass guard).
examples/hello_ipc.rs Minimal two-process hello over MessagePipe.
examples/brokered_open.rs Sandboxed child opens a file only via the broker.
examples/site_assignment.rs URLs -> SiteInstance -> process demo.
docs/architecture.md Architecture + process model narrative; the canonical diagram.
docs/threat-model.md Trust boundary, attacker model, defense-in-depth, blast radius.
docs/security-policy.md Capability profiles + baseline seccomp policy reference.
docs/ipc-protocol.md Wire framing, MsgKind, handle-passing protocol.
templates/rootfs/manifest.toml Minimal renderer rootfs contents for pivot_root.

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Single source-of-truth schedule for Phases 0–13 (doc headings ## 12–## 25). Durations sum to the Section 1 timeline (~30 engineer-weeks, 1 staff eng, Linux-primary). Exit criteria are measurable + CI-gated; security phases gate on the sbx attack red-team harness reporting zero escapes.

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria (measurable)
0 (## 12) Workspace: 12 sbx-* crate shells, sbx-core types, sbx CLI, CI/Makefile/Dockerfile; Linux primary; hello-Message smoke test 1.5 wk Over-scoping shells; UDS env quirks in CI containers cargo build --workspace + clippy -D warnings green; sbx ipc-ping: browser spawns child, exchanges 1 hello Message over UDS, child exits 0; CI runs in Docker
1 (## 13) sbx-ipc core: MessagePipe over UDS; Message{header,payload} framing/serialization; req/response + async; Interface+Endpoint<T> typed binding 2.5 wk Wire-format ambiguity; head-of-line blocking; req_id reuse Round-trip 10⁶ msgs lossless; framing fuzzed (no panic on truncated/oversized header); typed Endpoint<T> req→resp p50 <20 µs loopback; no handle passing yet
2 (## 14) Handle + SHM passing: FD via SCM_RIGHTS; ownership/close semantics; memfd SharedBuffer+Mapping (ro/rw)+seals; ShmRing zero-copy 2 wk FD leaks across boundary; double-close; missing F_SEAL_*→TOCTOU on size; partial cmsg recv Pass FD over UDS, peer reads same inode; Handle drop closes exactly once (leak test: fd count stable over 10⁵ passes); ShmRing SPSC throughput ≥5 GB/s; F_SEAL_SHRINK|GROW|WRITE enforced
3 (## 15) sbx-launcher: clone/exec child w/ typed ProcessType; IPC channel+args+env; handshake; SIGCHLD reap; ProcessHandle+restart hooks (no sandbox/zygote) 2 wk Zombie reaping races; fd inheritance leaks to child; handshake deadlock Spawn each ProcessType, complete handshake, clean exit; no zombies after 10³ spawn/exit cycles; only the channel fd inherited (verified via /proc/<pid>/fd); reap latency observed <1 ms
4 (## 16) Linux sandbox pt.1: unpriv USER ns; PID/NET/MNT/IPC/UTS ns; pivot_root to minimal rootfs; rlimits; PR_SET_NO_NEW_PRIVS; drop all caps 3 wk USER-ns disabled on host (kernel config); pivot_root mount-propagation pitfalls; incomplete cap drop Child in all 6 ns (verified via /proc/<pid>/ns/* distinct from broker); NET ns has no routable iface; CapEff=0000000000000000; NoNewPrivs:1; rootfs read-only, host / invisible post-pivot_root
5 (## 17) Linux sandbox pt.2: SyscallPolicy DSL→SeccompFilter cBPF over seccomp_data; Allow/Errno/Trap/Kill + arg filters; arch check (block x32/ia32); baseline renderer policy; SIGSYS handling; two-layer model 3 wk Missing arch check → ABI bypass; over-permissive baseline; filter too strict bricks renderer; cBPF jump-offset errors sbx attack syscall suite: 0 forbidden syscalls succeed (each → ERRNO/KILL_PROCESS); arch != AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64 killed (x32/ia32 bypass blocked); filter installs only after no_new_privs; baseline renderer runs end-to-end under policy
6 (## 18) sbx-zygote: pre-forked pre-sandboxed template; ForkServer protocol; COW page sharing; per-child seccomp seal/tighten; sbx-launcher use_zygote 2.5 wk COW pages diverge → memory blowup; stale fds shared into child; unsealed policy in forked child Zygote-spawned renderer p50 spawn <10 ms (vs fresh-exec baseline, ≥3× faster); COW: per-renderer private RSS <5 MB over shared; each forked child seals tightened seccomp before untrusted work (verified by post-seal sbx attack)
7 (## 19) sbx-broker: FileBroker/SocketBroker/ResourceBroker; renderer-asks-broker; PolicyEngine validates BrokerRequest vs caller CapabilitySet; brokered FDs over IPC; deny-by-default 2.5 wk TOCTOU on path validation; confused-deputy (broker over-trusts caller); path traversal/symlink escapes; default-allow leak Renderer (NET ns, no fs caps) opens a permitted file ONLY via broker; out-of-CapabilitySet request → Denied (100% of sbx attack broker cases); path canonicalized, ../symlink escapes refused; unknown op denied by default
8 (## 20) Capability model: Capability/CapabilitySet; per-ProcessType default profiles (renderer minimal; net/GPU relaxed); grant/revoke; least-privilege enforcement points; principal=SiteInstance 1.5 wk Profile drift to over-privilege; revoke not honored mid-session; ambient authority leak Renderer default profile holds no OpenFile/ConnectSocket; each profile audited against least-privilege checklist; revoked Capability → subsequent broker op Denied; principal resolves to SiteInstance
9 (## 21) Site isolation: SiteInstance/BrowsingInstance; site = scheme+eTLD+1 via public-suffix list; process-per-site-instance; site→process map; ProcessReusePolicy+limits; cross-origin framing 2 wk PSL parse errors → wrong site boundary; cross-site sharing leak; unbounded process growth site_for(url) correct on PSL conformance vectors (incl. multi-label suffixes *.ck, private domains); two distinct sites → distinct processes; same BrowsingInstance related sites may co-reside; LimitReached→ReuseExistingForSite past cap
10 (## 22) Browser orchestrator: ProcessHost registry; RenderProcessHost; RouteId IPC router (multiplex frames over one pipe); navigation→SiteInstance→process assignment; wire launcher+broker+site+crash 2.5 wk RouteId misroute/collision; registry leak on child exit; navigation→assignment race N frames multiplexed over 1 pipe, zero cross-RouteId delivery; navigation assigns correct SiteInstance/process; ProcessHost entry removed on child exit (no leak over 10³ navs); end-to-end nav→render→broker works
11 (## 23) Crash handling: detect crash (SIGCHLD/exit/IPC disconnect); capture Minidump artifact; blast-radius isolation; RestartPolicy; "sad tab" signal; crash reporting 2 wk Missed crash signal; restart storm; cascading failure across renderers; lost minidump Kill a renderer (sbx crash-test): browser + sibling renderers survive (renderer death != browser death); Minidump(pid/signal/maps) captured; "sad tab" signaled; Restart{max,window} caps restart storms; crash detected <100 ms
12 (## 24) Resource mgmt + observability: per-process mem/CPU rlimits + budgets; priority; ProcessReusePolicy under memory pressure; metrics (proc count, crash rate, IPC throughput, broker rate, spawn latency); cross-boundary tracing 1.5 wk rlimit OOM-kills wrong process; metric overhead skews latency; trace context lost across boundary Per-process RLIMIT_AS/CPU enforced (over-budget renderer constrained, browser unaffected); all 5 metrics exported; tracing spans cross trust boundary via IPC; metrics overhead <2% on spawn/IPC paths
13 (## 25) Security validation: sbx attack red-team harness (forbidden syscalls + file/socket → MUST be denied/killed); seccomp conformance; IPC fuzzing (malformed Message never crashes broker); spawn+IPC benchmarks vs targets 2.5 wk Untested escape vector; fuzzer finds broker panic; benchmark regression hidden sbx attack: 0 escapes (every forbidden syscall killed/ERRNO; every out-of-cap file/socket Denied); seccomp policy 100% conformance; IPC fuzz ≥10⁶ malformed msgs, 0 broker crashes/panics; spawn <10 ms + IPC throughput targets met. Security "done" gate.

Timeline total: 1.5+2.5+2+2+3+3+2.5+2.5+1.5+2+2.5+2+1.5+2.5 = 31.5 engineer-weeks (≈ Section 1's ~30-wk estimate + buffer).

"Phase-0 done" vs "full done": Phase-0 done = the workspace compiles, the 12 sbx-* crates + sbx-core types + sbx CLI exist as shells, and the smoke test passes: the privileged browser process spawns one child that exchanges a hello Message over a UNIX domain socket and exits cleanly — proving the multi-process plumbing without any sandbox, broker, zygote, or site isolation. Full done = all 14 phases land and the security gate (Phase 13) is green: an untrusted renderer runs confined behind two independent layers (seccomp-bpf syscall filter AND the broker PolicyEngine), every OS resource access is either denied by seccomp or brokered + validated against the renderer's CapabilitySet, site isolation places distinct sites in distinct processes, a renderer crash is contained to a "sad tab" (browser + siblings unaffected), and the sbx attack red-team harness reports zero sandbox escapes and zero privilege escalations — with spawn-latency, IPC-throughput, and per-process-memory targets met. Consistent with Section 1: this delivers the process model + the sandbox, not a web engine (the renderer is a minimal untrusted stand-in; real rendering is #260/#305). macOS (Seatbelt) + Windows (job/restricted-token/AppContainer) backends remain PhaseDeferred stubs.
